import math
import torch
from torch import nn
import torch.nn.functional as F


def fused_leaky_relu(input, bias, negative_slope=0.2, scale=2 ** 0.5):
    return FusedLeakyReLUFunction.apply(input, bias, negative_slope, scale)


class FusedLeakyReLUFunctionBackward(Function):

    @staticmethod
    def forward(ctx, grad_output, out, negative_slope, scale):
        ctx.save_for_backward(out)
        ctx.negative_slope = negative_slope
        ctx.scale = scale
        empty = grad_output.new_empty(0)
        grad_input = fused.fused_bias_act(grad_output, empty, out, 3, 1,
            negative_slope, scale)
        dim = [0]
        if grad_input.ndim > 2:
            dim += list(range(2, grad_input.ndim))
        grad_bias = grad_input.sum(dim).detach()
        return grad_input, grad_bias

    @staticmethod
    def backward(ctx, gradgrad_input, gradgrad_bias):
        out, = ctx.saved_tensors
        gradgrad_out = fused.fused_bias_act(gradgrad_input, gradgrad_bias,
            out, 3, 1, ctx.negative_slope, ctx.scale)
        return gradgrad_out, None, None, None


class FusedLeakyReLUFunction(Function):

    @staticmethod
    def forward(ctx, input, bias, negative_slope, scale):
        empty = input.new_empty(0)
        out = fused.fused_bias_act(input, bias, empty, 3, 0, negative_slope,
            scale)
        ctx.save_for_backward(out)
        ctx.negative_slope = negative_slope
        ctx.scale = scale
        return out

    @staticmethod
    def backward(ctx, grad_output):
        out, = ctx.saved_tensors
        grad_input, grad_bias = FusedLeakyReLUFunctionBackward.apply(
            grad_output, out, ctx.negative_slope, ctx.scale)
        return grad_input, grad_bias, None, None


class EqualLinear(nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, in_dim, out_dim, bias=True, bias_init=0, lr_mul=1,
        activation=None):
        super().__init__()
        self.weight = nn.Parameter(torch.randn(out_dim, in_dim).div_(lr_mul))
        if bias:
            self.bias = nn.Parameter(torch.zeros(out_dim).fill_(bias_init))
        else:
            self.bias = None
        self.activation = activation
        self.scale = 1 / math.sqrt(in_dim) * lr_mul
        self.lr_mul = lr_mul

    def forward(self, input):
        if self.activation:
            out = F.linear(input, self.weight * self.scale)
            out = fused_leaky_relu(out, self.bias * self.lr_mul)
        else:
            out = F.linear(input, self.weight * self.scale, bias=self.bias *
                self.lr_mul)
        return out

    def __repr__(self):
        return (
            f'{self.__class__.__name__}({self.weight.shape[1]}, {self.weight.shape[0]})'
            )


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'in_dim': 4, 'out_dim': 4}]

    33	Develop within the project
    34	--------------------------
    35	
    36	Since 2020-08-14, this repository follows these `development guidelines <https://nvie.com/posts/a-successful-git-branching-model/>`_. The git flow is thus the following:
    37	
    38	.. figure:: https://nvie.com/img/git-model@2x.png
    39	    :alt: the Git branching model 
    40	    :width: 70%
    41	    
    42	    The git branching model
    43	
    44	Please consider using the :code:`--no-ff` option when merging to keep the repository consistent with PR. 
    45	
    46	In the project to adapt to :code:`JupyterLab` IntelSense, we decided to explicitly write the `return` statement for every function.
    47	
    48	As we are holding a single documentation page, we need to provide the users with version informations. When a new function or class is created please use the `Deprecated <https://pypi.org/project/Deprecated/>`__ lib to specify that the feature is new in the documentation. 
    49	
    50	.. code-block:: python
    51	
    52	    from deprecated.sphinx import deprecated
    53	    from deprecated.sphinx import versionadded
    54	    from deprecated.sphinx import versionchanged
    55	
    56	
    57	    @versionadded(version='1.0', reason="This function is new")
    58	    def function_one():
    59	        '''This is the function one'''
    60	
    61	
    62	    @versionchanged(version='1.0', reason="This function is modified")
    63	    def function_two():
    64	        '''This is the function two'''
    65	
    66	
    67	    @deprecated(version='1.0', reason="This function will be removed soon")
    68	    def function_three():
    69	        '''This is the function three'''
    70	    
    71	How to commit
    72	-------------
    73	
    74	In this repository we use the Conventional Commits specification.
    75	The Conventional Commits specification is a lightweight convention on top of commit messages. It provides an easy set of rules for creating an explicit commit history; which makes it easier to write automated tools on top of. This convention dovetails with SemVer, by describing the features, fixes, and breaking changes made in commit messages.
    76	
    77	You can learn more about Conventional Commits following this `link <https://www.conventionalcommits.org/en/v1.0.0/>`_
    78	
    79	What can I push and where
    80	-------------------------
    81	
    82	Our branching system embed some rules to avoid crash of the production environment. If you want to contribute to this framework, here are some basic rules that we try our best to follow :
    83	
    84	-   the modification you offer is solving a critical bug in prod : **PR in hotfix**
    85	-   the modification you propose solve the following issues : test, documentation, typo, quality, refactoring, translation **PR in master**
    86	-   the modification you propose is a new feature : open an issue to discuss with the maintainers and then **PR to develop**
    87	
    88	the maintainers will try their best to use PR for new features, to help the community follow the development, for other modification they will simply push to the appropriate branch
    89	
    90	Create a new release
    91	--------------------
    92	
    93	.. danger:: 
    94	
    95	    for maintainers only 
    96	    
    97	 .. warning::
    98	 
    99	     You need to use the :code:`commitizen` lib to create your release: `<https://commitizen-tools.github.io/commitizen>`_
   100	    
   101	In the files change the version number by runnning commitizen `bump`: 
   102	
   103	.. code-block:: console
   104	
   105	    cz bump
   106	
   107	It should modify for you the version number in :code:`sepal_ui/__init__.py`, :code:`setup.py`, and :code:`.cz.yaml` according to sementic versionning thanks to the conventional commit that we use in the lib. 
   108	
   109	It will also update the :code:`CHANGELOG.md` file with the latest commits, sorted by categories if you run the following code, using the version bumped in the previous commit.
   110	
   111	.. code-block:: console 
   112	
   113	    cz changelog --unreleased-version="v_x.x.x"
   114	
   115	Then push the current :code:`master` branch to the :code:`release` branch. You can now create a new tag with your new version number. use the same convention as the one found in :code:`.cz.yaml`: :code:`v_$minor.$major.$patch$prerelease`.
   116	
   117	.. warning::
   118	
   119	    The target branch of the new release is :code:`release` not :code:`master`. 
   120	    
   121	The CI should take everything in control from here and execute the :code:`Upload Python Package` GitHub Action that is publishing the new version on `PyPi <https://badge.fury.io/py/sepal-ui>`_.
   122	    
   123	Once it's done you need to trigger the rebuild of SEPAL. modify the following `file <https://github.com/openforis/sepal/blob/master/modules/sandbox/docker/script/init_sepal_ui.sh>`_ with the latest version number and the rebuild will start automatically. 
   124	
   125	
   126	Setting ENV variables
   127	---------------------
   128	
   129	Sometimes is useful to create enviromental variables to store some data that your workflows will receive (i.e. component testing). For example, to perform the local tests of the :code:`planetapi` sepal module, the :code:`PLANET_API_KEY` and :code:`PLANET_API_CREDENTIALS` env vars are required, even though they are also skippable.
   130	
   131	To store a variable in your local session, just type :code:`export=` followed by the var value.
   132	
   133	.. code-block:: console 
   134	
   135	    $ export PLANET_API_KEY="neverending_resourcesapi"
   136	    
   137	However, this variable will expire everytime you start a new session, to create it every session and make it live longer, go to your :code:`home` folder and save the previous line in the :code:`.bash_profile` file.
   138	    
   139	.. code-block:: console 
   140	
   141	    $ vim .bash_profile
   142	
   143	.. note::
   144	    The current enviromental keys and its structure is the following:
   145	
   146	    - PLANET_API_CREDENTIALS: '{"username": "user@neim.com", "password": "secure"}'
   147	    - PLANET_API_KEY: "string_planet_api_key"
   148	
   149	.. spelling:: 
   150	
   151	    pre
   152

import math
import torch
from torch import nn
import torch.nn.functional as F


def fused_leaky_relu(input, bias, negative_slope=0.2, scale=2 ** 0.5):
    return FusedLeakyReLUFunction.apply(input, bias, negative_slope, scale)


def make_kernel(k):
    k = torch.tensor(k, dtype=torch.float32)
    if k.ndim == 1:
        k = k[None, :] * k[:, None]
    k /= k.sum()
    return k


def upfirdn2d(input, kernel, up=1, down=1, pad=(0, 0)):
    out = UpFirDn2d.apply(input, kernel, (up, up), (down, down), (pad[0],
        pad[1], pad[0], pad[1]))
    return out


class FusedLeakyReLUFunctionBackward(Function):

    @staticmethod
    def forward(ctx, grad_output, out, negative_slope, scale):
        ctx.save_for_backward(out)
        ctx.negative_slope = negative_slope
        ctx.scale = scale
        empty = grad_output.new_empty(0)
        grad_input = fused.fused_bias_act(grad_output, empty, out, 3, 1,
            negative_slope, scale)
        dim = [0]
        if grad_input.ndim > 2:
            dim += list(range(2, grad_input.ndim))
        grad_bias = grad_input.sum(dim).detach()
        return grad_input, grad_bias

    @staticmethod
    def backward(ctx, gradgrad_input, gradgrad_bias):
        out, = ctx.saved_tensors
        gradgrad_out = fused.fused_bias_act(gradgrad_input, gradgrad_bias,
            out, 3, 1, ctx.negative_slope, ctx.scale)
        return gradgrad_out, None, None, None


class FusedLeakyReLUFunction(Function):

    @staticmethod
    def forward(ctx, input, bias, negative_slope, scale):
        empty = input.new_empty(0)
        out = fused.fused_bias_act(input, bias, empty, 3, 0, negative_slope,
            scale)
        ctx.save_for_backward(out)
        ctx.negative_slope = negative_slope
        ctx.scale = scale
        return out

    @staticmethod
    def backward(ctx, grad_output):
        out, = ctx.saved_tensors
        grad_input, grad_bias = FusedLeakyReLUFunctionBackward.apply(
            grad_output, out, ctx.negative_slope, ctx.scale)
        return grad_input, grad_bias, None, None


class EqualLinear(nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, in_dim, out_dim, bias=True, bias_init=0, lr_mul=1,
        activation=None):
        super().__init__()
        self.weight = nn.Parameter(torch.randn(out_dim, in_dim).div_(lr_mul))
        if bias:
            self.bias = nn.Parameter(torch.zeros(out_dim).fill_(bias_init))
        else:
            self.bias = None
        self.activation = activation
        self.scale = 1 / math.sqrt(in_dim) * lr_mul
        self.lr_mul = lr_mul

    def forward(self, input):
        if self.activation:
            out = F.linear(input, self.weight * self.scale)
            out = fused_leaky_relu(out, self.bias * self.lr_mul)
        else:
            out = F.linear(input, self.weight * self.scale, bias=self.bias *
                self.lr_mul)
        return out

    def __repr__(self):
        return (
            f'{self.__class__.__name__}({self.weight.shape[1]}, {self.weight.shape[0]})'
            )


class UpFirDn2dBackward(Function):

    @staticmethod
    def forward(ctx, grad_output, kernel, grad_kernel, up, down, pad, g_pad,
        in_size, out_size):
        up_x, up_y = up
        down_x, down_y = down
        g_pad_x0, g_pad_x1, g_pad_y0, g_pad_y1 = g_pad
        grad_output = grad_output.reshape(-1, out_size[0], out_size[1], 1)
        grad_input = upfirdn2d_op.upfirdn2d(grad_output, grad_kernel,
            down_x, down_y, up_x, up_y, g_pad_x0, g_pad_x1, g_pad_y0, g_pad_y1)
        grad_input = grad_input.view(in_size[0], in_size[1], in_size[2],
            in_size[3])
        ctx.save_for_backward(kernel)
        pad_x0, pad_x1, pad_y0, pad_y1 = pad
        ctx.up_x = up_x
        ctx.up_y = up_y
        ctx.down_x = down_x
        ctx.down_y = down_y
        ctx.pad_x0 = pad_x0
        ctx.pad_x1 = pad_x1
        ctx.pad_y0 = pad_y0
        ctx.pad_y1 = pad_y1
        ctx.in_size = in_size
        ctx.out_size = out_size
        return grad_input

    @staticmethod
    def backward(ctx, gradgrad_input):
        kernel, = ctx.saved_tensors
        gradgrad_input = gradgrad_input.reshape(-1, ctx.in_size[2], ctx.
            in_size[3], 1)
        gradgrad_out = upfirdn2d_op.upfirdn2d(gradgrad_input, kernel, ctx.
            up_x, ctx.up_y, ctx.down_x, ctx.down_y, ctx.pad_x0, ctx.pad_x1,
            ctx.pad_y0, ctx.pad_y1)
        gradgrad_out = gradgrad_out.view(ctx.in_size[0], ctx.in_size[1],
            ctx.out_size[0], ctx.out_size[1])
        return gradgrad_out, None, None, None, None, None, None, None, None


class UpFirDn2d(Function):

    @staticmethod
    def forward(ctx, input, kernel, up, down, pad):
        up_x, up_y = up
        down_x, down_y = down
        pad_x0, pad_x1, pad_y0, pad_y1 = pad
        kernel_h, kernel_w = kernel.shape
        _batch, channel, in_h, in_w = input.shape
        ctx.in_size = input.shape
        input = input.reshape(-1, in_h, in_w, 1)
        ctx.save_for_backward(kernel, torch.flip(kernel, [0, 1]))
        out_h = (in_h * up_y + pad_y0 + pad_y1 - kernel_h) // down_y + 1
        out_w = (in_w * up_x + pad_x0 + pad_x1 - kernel_w) // down_x + 1
        ctx.out_size = out_h, out_w
        ctx.up = up_x, up_y
        ctx.down = down_x, down_y
        ctx.pad = pad_x0, pad_x1, pad_y0, pad_y1
        g_pad_x0 = kernel_w - pad_x0 - 1
        g_pad_y0 = kernel_h - pad_y0 - 1
        g_pad_x1 = in_w * up_x - out_w * down_x + pad_x0 - up_x + 1
        g_pad_y1 = in_h * up_y - out_h * down_y + pad_y0 - up_y + 1
        ctx.g_pad = g_pad_x0, g_pad_x1, g_pad_y0, g_pad_y1
        out = upfirdn2d_op.upfirdn2d(input, kernel, up_x, up_y, down_x,
            down_y, pad_x0, pad_x1, pad_y0, pad_y1)
        out = out.view(-1, channel, out_h, out_w)
        return out

    @staticmethod
    def backward(ctx, grad_output):
        kernel, grad_kernel = ctx.saved_tensors
        grad_input = UpFirDn2dBackward.apply(grad_output, kernel,
            grad_kernel, ctx.up, ctx.down, ctx.pad, ctx.g_pad, ctx.in_size,
            ctx.out_size)
        return grad_input, None, None, None, None


class Blur(nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, kernel, pad, upsample_factor=1):
        super().__init__()
        kernel = make_kernel(kernel)
        if upsample_factor > 1:
            kernel = kernel * upsample_factor ** 2
        self.register_buffer('kernel', kernel)
        self.pad = pad

    def forward(self, input):
        out = upfirdn2d(input, self.kernel, pad=self.pad)
        return out

    def __init__(self, in_channel, out_channel, kernel_size, style_dim,

</think>To calculate the remainder of \(14^{27} \mod 55\) using repeated squaring, follow these steps:

1. **Binary Decomposition of the Exponent**:
   - Convert the exponent 27 to binary: \(27_{10} = 11011_2\).
   - This binary representation tells us that \(27 = 16 + 8 + 2 + 1\).

2. **Compute Powers Modulo 55**:
   - Start with the base:
     \[
     14^1 \mod 55 = 14
     \]
   - Compute \(14^2 \mod 55\):
     \[
     14^2 = 196 \quad \text{and} \quad 196 \mod 55 = 31
     \]
   - Compute \(14^4 \mod 55\):
     \[
     14^4 = (14^2)^2 = 31^2 = 961 \quad \text{and} \quad 961 \mod 55 = 26
     \]
   - Compute \(14^8 \mod 55\):
     \[
     14^8 = (14^4)^2 = 26^2 = 676 \quad \text{and} \quad 676 \mod 55 = 16
     \]
   - Compute \(14^{16} \mod 55\):
     \[
     14^{16} = (14^8)^2 = 16^2 = 256 \quad \text{and} \quad 256 \mod 55 = 36
     \]

3. **Multiply Relevant Powers Modulo 55**:
   - Combine the results for the exponents corresponding to the binary digits that are 1 (i.e., 16, 8, 2, and 1):
     \[
     14^{27} \mod 55 = (14^{16} \times 14^8 \times 14^2 \times 14^1) \mod 55
     \]
   - Compute step-by-step:
     \[
     (36 \times 16) \mod 55 = 576 \mod 55 = 26
     \]
     \[
     (26 \times 31) \mod 55 = 806 \mod 55 = 36
     \]
     \[
     (36 \times 14) \mod 55 = 504 \mod 55 = 9
     \]

Thus, the remainder of \(14^{27} \mod 55\) is \(\boxed{9}\).

Alternatively, perhaps the contamination level corresponds to the number of edges that form the boundary of the polygon that are crossed when moving from the cell to the polygon's interior. But this is not precise. 

An alternative approach inspired by computational geometry for this type of problem:

The polygon is strictly convex, so each edge is part of its convex hull. The contaminated cells' positions lie around the polygon edges. To reconstruct the polygon, we can consider that each edge must form a straight line of contiguous contaminated cells along its length. 

Wait here's a possible step: The set of contaminated cells must form contiguous line segments along the edges of the polygon. The line segments can be found by grouping together the contaminated cells into line segments, and then forming the polygon by taking the endpoints of these segments.

However, since contamination levels are from 1-3, this isn't straightforward. 

Alternatively, the vertices of the polygon will be the points where the direction of the contaminated segments changes. 

Suppose we trace all the edges as lines between contaminated points.

1. For each cell (x,y) in the contaminated list, look at its neighbors. The net direction of the contamination might indicate the edges' directions.

Another Idea: The edges of the polygon must be maximal straight lines through the contaminated cells, such that turning at vertices creates the convex polygon. 

Perhaps, to find edges, we can group contaminated cells into segments which form straight lines, then the vertices are the ends where direction changes. 

For example, considering the edge segments that form straight lines in any direction (not just axis-aligned), and these segments will form the edges of the convex polygon. The vertices are at the ends of these edges.  

This approach requires that each edge of the polygon corresponds to a maximal straight line segment of contaminated cells along it. 

But how do we find these segments? 

Perhaps first, collect all contaminated cells, then compute all lines between them that pass through the contaminated cells in a straight line. The longest such lines could form the edges of the polygon. The intersections of these lines would be the vertices.

class ModulatedConv2d(nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, in_channel, out_channel, kernel_size, style_dim,
        demodulate=True, upsample=False, downsample=False, blur_kernel=[1, 
        3, 3, 1]):
        super().__init__()
        self.eps = 1e-08
        self.kernel_size = kernel_size
        self.in_channel = in_channel
        self.out_channel = out_channel
        self.upsample = upsample
        self.downsample = downsample
        if upsample:
            factor = 2
            p = len(blur_kernel) - factor - (kernel_size - 1)
            pad0 = (p + 1) // 2 + factor - 1
            pad1 = p // 2 + 1
            self.blur = Blur(blur_kernel, pad=(pad0, pad1), upsample_factor
                =factor)
        if downsample:
            factor = 2
            p = len(blur_kernel) - factor + (kernel_size - 1)
            pad0 = (p + 1) // 2
            pad1 = p // 2
            self.blur = Blur(blur_kernel, pad=(pad0, pad1))
        fan_in = in_channel * kernel_size ** 2
        self.scale = 1 / math.sqrt(fan_in)
        self.padding = kernel_size // 2
        self.weight = nn.Parameter(torch.randn(1, out_channel, in_channel,
            kernel_size, kernel_size))
        self.modulation = EqualLinear(style_dim, in_channel, bias_init=1)
        self.demodulate = demodulate

    def __repr__(self):
        return (
            f'{self.__class__.__name__}({self.in_channel}, {self.out_channel}, {self.kernel_size}, upsample={self.upsample}, downsample={self.downsample})'
            )

    def forward(self, input, style):
        batch, in_channel, height, width = input.shape
        style = self.modulation(style).view(batch, 1, in_channel, 1, 1)
        weight = self.scale * self.weight * style
        if self.demodulate:
            demod = torch.rsqrt(weight.pow(2).sum([2, 3, 4]) + 1e-08)
            weight = weight * demod.view(batch, self.out_channel, 1, 1, 1)
        weight = weight.view(batch * self.out_channel, in_channel, self.
            kernel_size, self.kernel_size)
        if self.upsample:
            input = input.view(1, batch * in_channel, height, width)
            weight = weight.view(batch, self.out_channel, in_channel, self.
                kernel_size, self.kernel_size)
            weight = weight.transpose(1, 2).reshape(batch * in_channel,
                self.out_channel, self.kernel_size, self.kernel_size)
            out = F.conv_transpose2d(input, weight, padding=0, stride=2,
                groups=batch)
            _, _, height, width = out.shape
            out = out.view(batch, self.out_channel, height, width)
            out = self.blur(out)
        elif self.downsample:
            input = self.blur(input)
            _, _, height, width = input.shape
            input = input.view(1, batch * in_channel, height, width)
            out = F.conv2d(input, weight, padding=0, stride=2, groups=batch)
            _, _, height, width = out.shape
            out = out.view(batch, self.out_channel, height, width)
        else:
            input = input.view(1, batch * in_channel, height, width)
            out = F.conv2d(input, weight, padding=self.padding, groups=batch)
            _, _, height, width = out.shape
            out = out.view(batch, self.out_channel, height, width)
        return out


class Upsample(nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, kernel, factor=2):
        super().__init__()
        self.factor = factor
        kernel = make_kernel(kernel) * factor ** 2
        self.register_buffer('kernel', kernel)
        p = kernel.shape[0] - factor
        pad0 = (p + 1) // 2 + factor - 1
        pad1 = p // 2
        self.pad = pad0, pad1

    def forward(self, input):
        out = upfirdn2d(input, self.kernel, up=self.factor, down=1, pad=
            self.pad)
        return out


class ToRGB(nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, in_channel, style_dim, upsample=True, blur_kernel=[1,
        3, 3, 1]):
        super().__init__()
        if upsample:
            self.upsample = Upsample(blur_kernel)
        self.conv = ModulatedConv2d(in_channel, 3, 1, style_dim, demodulate
            =False)
        self.bias = nn.Parameter(torch.zeros(1, 3, 1, 1))

    def forward(self, input, style, skip=None):
        out = self.conv(input, style)
        out = out + self.bias
        if skip is not None:
            skip = self.upsample(skip)
            out = out + skip
        return out

    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'in_channel': 4, 'style_dim': 4}]

   <#sep#>from torch.autograd import Function
import math
import torch
from torch import nn
import torch.nn.functional as F


def fused_leaky_relu(input, bias, negative_slope=0.2, scale=2 ** 0.5):
    return FusedLeakyReLUFunction.apply(input, bias, negative_slope, scale)


def make_kernel(k):
    k = torch.tensor(k, dtype=torch.float32)
    if k.ndim == 1:
        k = k[None, :] * k[:, None]
    k /= k.sum()
    return k


def upfirdn2d(input, kernel, up=1, down=1, pad=(0, 0)):
    out = UpFirDn2d.apply(input, kernel, (up, up), (down, down), (pad[0],
        pad[1], pad[0], pad[1]))
    return out


class FusedLeakyReLUFunctionBackward(Function):

    @staticmethod
    def forward(ctx, grad_output, out, negative_slope, scale):
        ctx.save_for_backward(out)
        ctx.negative_slope = negative_slope
        ctx.scale = scale
        empty = grad_output.new_empty(0)
        grad_input = fused.fused_bias_act(grad_output, empty, out, 3, 1,
            negative_slope, scale)
        dim = [0]
        if grad_input.ndim > 2:
            dim += list(range(2, grad_input.ndim))
        grad_bias = grad_input.sum(dim).detach()
        return grad_input, grad_bias

    @staticmethod
    def backward(ctx, gradgrad_input, gradgrad_bias):
        out, = ctx.saved_tensors
        gradgrad_out = fused.fused_bias_act(gradgrad_input, gradgrad_bias,
            out, 3, 1, ctx.negative_slope, ctx.scale)
        return gradgrad_out, None, None, None


class FusedLeakyReLUFunction(Function):

    @staticmethod
    def forward(ctx, input, bias, negative_slope, scale):
        empty = input.new_empty(0)
        out = fused.fused_bias_act(input, bias, empty, 3, 0, negative_slope,
            scale)
        ctx.save_for_backward(out)
        ctx.negative_slope = negative_slope
        ctx.scale = scale
        return out

    @staticmethod
    def backward(ctx, grad_output):
        out, = ctx.saved_tensors
        grad_input, grad_bias = FusedLeakyReLUFunctionBackward.apply(
            grad_output, out, ctx.negative_slope, ctx.scale)
        return grad_input, grad_bias, None, None


class EqualLinear(nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, in_dim, out_dim, bias=True, bias_init=0, lr_mul=1,
        activation=None):
        super().__init__()
        self.weight = nn.Parameter(torch.randn(out_dim, in_dim).div_(lr_mul))
        if bias:
            self.bias = nn.Parameter(torch.zeros(out_dim).fill_(bias_init))
        else:
            self.bias = None
        self.activation = activation
        self.scale = 1 / math.sqrt(in_dim) * lr_mul
        self.lr_mul = lr_mul

    def forward(self, input):
        if self.activation:
            out = F.linear(input, self.weight * self.scale)
            out = fused_leaky_relu(out, self.bias * self.lr_mul)
        else:
            out = F.linear(input, self.weight * self.scale, bias=self.bias *
                self.lr_mul)
        return out

    def __repr__(self):
        return (
            f'{self.__class__.__name__}({self.weight.shape[1]}, {self.weight.shape[0]})'
            )


class UpFirDn2dBackward(Function):

    @staticmethod
    def forward(ctx, grad_output, kernel, grad_kernel, up, down, pad, g_pad,
        in_size, out_size):
        up_x, up_y = up
        down_x, down_y = down
        g_pad_x0, g_pad_x1, g_pad_y0, g_pad_y1 = g_pad
        grad_output = grad_output.reshape(-1, out_size[0], out_size[1], 1)
        grad_input = upfirdn2d_op.upfirdn2d(grad_output, grad_kernel,
            down_x, down_y, up_x, up_y, g_pad_x0, g_pad_x1, g_pad_y0, g_pad_y1)
        grad_input = grad_input.view(in_size[0], in_size[1], in_size[2],
            in_size[3])
        ctx.save_for_backward(kernel)
        pad_x0, pad_x1, pad_y0, pad_y1 = pad
        ctx.up_x = up_x
        ctx.up_y = up_y
        ctx.down_x = down_x
        ctx.down_y = down_y
        ctx.pad_x0 = pad_x0
        ctx.pad_x1 = pad_x1
        ctx.pad_y0 = pad_y0
        ctx.pad_y1 = pad_y1
        ctx.in_size = in_size
        ctx.out_size = out_size
        return grad_input

    @staticmethod
    def backward(ctx, gradgrad_input):
        kernel, = ctx.saved_tensors
        gradgrad_input = gradgrad_input.reshape(-1, ctx.in_size[2], ctx.
            in_size[3], 1)
        gradgrad_out = upfirdn2d_op.upfirdn2d(gradgrad_input, kernel, ctx.
            up_x, ctx.up_y, ctx.down_x, ctx.down_y, ctx.pad_x0, ctx.pad_x1,
            ctx.pad_y0, ctx.pad_y1)
        gradgrad_out = gradgrad_out.view(ctx.in_size[0], ctx.in_size[1],
            ctx.out_size[0], ctx.out_size[1])
        return gradgrad_out, None, None, None, None, None, None, None, None


class UpFirDn2d(Function):

    @staticmethod
    def forward(ctx, input, kernel, up, down, pad):
        up_x, up_y = up
        down_x, down_y = down
        pad_x0, pad_x1, pad_y0, pad_y1 = pad
        kernel_h, kernel_w = kernel.shape
        _batch, channel, in_h, in_w = input.shape
        ctx.in_size = input.shape
        input = input.reshape(-1, in_h, in_w, 1)
        ctx.save_for_backward(kernel, torch.flip(kernel, [0, 1]))
        out_h = (in_h * up_y + pad_y0 + pad_y1 - kernel_h) // down_y + 1
        out_w = (in_w * up_x + pad_x0 + pad_x1 - kernel_w) // down_x + 1
        ctx.out_size = out_h, out_w
        ctx.up = up_x, up_y
        ctx.down = down_x, down_y
        ctx.pad = pad_x0, pad_x1, pad_y0, pad_y1
        g_pad_x0 = kernel_w - pad_x0 - 1
        g_pad_y0 = kernel_h - pad_y0 - 1
        g_pad_x1 = in_w * up_x - out_w * down_x + pad_x0 - up_x + 1
        g_pad_y1 = in_h * up_y - out_h * down_y + pad_y0 - up_y + 1
        ctx.g_pad = g_pad_x0, g_pad_x1, g_pad_y0, g_pad_y1
        out = upfirdn2d_op.upfirdn2d(input, kernel, up_x, up_y, down_x,
            down_y, pad_x0, pad_x1, pad_y0, pad_y1)
        out = out.view(-1, channel, out_h, out_w)
        return out

    @staticmethod
    def backward(ctx, grad_output):
        kernel, grad_kernel = ctx.saved_tensors
        grad_input = UpFirDn2dBackward.apply(grad_output, kernel,
            grad_kernel, ctx.up, ctx.down, ctx.pad, ctx.g_pad, ctx.in_size,
            ctx.out_size)
        return grad_input, None, None, None, None


class Blur(nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, kernel, pad, upsample_factor=1):
        super().__init__()
        kernel = make_kernel(kernel)
        if upsample_factor > 1:
            kernel = kernel * upsample_factor ** 2
        self.register_buffer('kernel', kernel)
        self.pad = pad

    def forward(self, input):
        out = upfirdn2d(input, self.kernel, pad=self.pad)
        return out

    def __init__(self, in_channel, out_channel, kernel_size, style_dim,
        demodulate=True, upsample=False, downsample<#sep#> interval, then what is the boundary?

class ModulatedConv2d(nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, in_channel, out_channel, kernel_size, style_dim,
        demodulate=True, upsample=False, downsample=False, blur_kernel=[1, 
        3, 3, 1]):
        super().__init__()
        self.eps = 1e-08
        self.kernel_size = kernel_size
        self.in_channel = in_channel
        self.out_channel = out_channel
        self.upsample = upsample
        self.downsample = downsample
        if upsample:
            factor = 2
            p = len(blur_kernel) - factor - (kernel_size - 1)
            pad0 = (p + 1) // 2 + factor - 1
            pad1 = p // 2 + 1
            self.blur = Blur(blur_kernel, pad=(pad0, pad1), upsample_factor
                =factor)
        if downsample:
            factor = 2
            p = len(blur_kernel) - factor + (kernel_size - 1)
            pad0 = (p + 1) // 2
            pad1 = p // 2
            self.blur = Blur(blur_kernel, pad=(pad0, pad1))
        fan_in = in_channel * kernel_size ** 2
        self.scale = 1 / math.sqrt(fan_in)
        self.padding = kernel_size // 2
        self.weight = nn.Parameter(torch.randn(1, out_channel, in_channel,
            kernel_size, kernel_size))
        self.modulation = EqualLinear(style_dim, in_channel, bias_init=1)
        self.demodulate = demodulate

    def __repr__(self):
        return (
            f'{self.__class__.__name__}({self.in_channel}, {self.out_channel}, {self.kernel_size}, upsample={self.upsample}, downsample={self.downsample})'
            )

    def forward(self, input, style):
        batch, in_channel, height, width = input.shape
        style = self.modulation(style).view(batch, 1, in_channel, 1, 1)
        weight = self.scale * self.weight * style
        if self.demodulate:
            demod = torch.rsqrt(weight.pow(2).sum([2, 3, 4]) + 1e-08)
            weight = weight * demod.view(batch, self.out_channel, 1, 1, 1)
        weight = weight.view(batch * self.out_channel, in_channel, self.
            kernel_size, self.kernel_size)
        if self.upsample:
            input = input.view(1, batch * in_channel, height, width)
            weight = weight.view(batch, self.out_channel, in_channel, self.
                kernel_size, self.kernel_size)
            weight = weight.transpose(1, 2).reshape(batch * in_channel,
                self.out_channel, self.kernel_size, self.kernel_size)
            out = F.conv_transpose2d(input, weight, padding=0, stride=2,
                groups=batch)
            _, _, height, width = out.shape
            out = out.view(batch, self.out_channel, height, width)
            out = self.blur(out)
        elif self.downsample:
            input = self.blur(input)
            _, _, height, width = input.shape
            input = input.view(1, batch * in_channel, height, width)
            out = F.conv2d(input, weight, padding=0, stride=2, groups=batch)
            _, _, height, width = out.shape
            out = out.view(batch, self.out_channel, height, width)
        else:
            input = input.view(1, batch * in_channel, height, width)
            out = F.conv2d(input, weight, padding=self.padding, groups=batch)
            _, _, height, width = out.shape
            out = out.view(batch, self.out_channel, height, width)
        return out


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'in_channel': 4, 'out_channel': 4, 'kernel_size': 4,
        'style_dim': 4}]
<#sep#>}, where F is the finite set of points removed. But the problem states "a finite number of points" without specifying F, so maybe in the problem's context, F is understood, but since it's not given, perhaps the answer is stated as the union of the boundary of B and those points. However, in standard mathematical notation, we can present it as ∂B ∪ F, so I think that's acceptable.

Wait, but maybe in the problem's perspective, since you are removing points from the open ball, which is a subset of ℝⁿ, and the boundary operation is topological. Therefore, the answer should be the original boundary plus those points. So yes, I think that's the correct answer.
</think>To find the boundary of an open ball \( B \) in \( \mathbb{R}^n \) minus a finite number of points \( F \), we proceed as follows:

1. **Identify the Boundary of the Open Ball:**
   The boundary of an open ball \( B \) in \( \mathbb{R}^n \) is the \((n-1)\)-dimensional sphere, denoted by \( \partial B \).

2. **Define the Set \( B \setminus F \):**
   Let \( F \) be a finite set of points removed from \( B \). The set \( B \setminus F \) is still an open set because removing a finite number of points from an open set in \( \mathbb{R}^n \) (where \( n \geq 2 \)) remains open.

3. **Determine the Closure of \( B \setminus F \):**
   The closure of \( B \setminus F \) is the same as the closure of \( B \), which is the closed ball. This is because removing a finite number of points from \( B \) does not affect the limit points of \( B \).

4. **Determine the Interior of \( B \setminus F \):**
   The interior of \( B \setminus F \) is \( B \setminus F \) itself, since \( B \setminus F \) is an open set.

5. **Compute the Boundary of \( B \setminus F \):**
   The boundary of a set \( A \) is given by the closure of \( A \) minus the interior of \( A \). Therefore, the boundary of \( B \setminus F \) is:
   \[
   \partial (B \setminus F) = \text{closure}(B \setminus F) \setminus \text{interior}(B \setminus F)
   \]
   Substituting the known values:
   \[
   \partial (B \setminus F) = \text{closure}(B) \setminus (B \setminus F)
   \]
   Since \( \text{closure}(B) \) is the closed ball, we have:
   \[
   \partial (B \setminus F) = (\text{closed ball}) \setminus (B \setminus F)
   \]
   The closed ball is the union of the open ball \( B \) and its boundary \( \partial B \):
   \[
   \partial (B \setminus F) = (B \cup \partial B) \setminus (B \setminus F)
   \]
   Simplifying the expression:
   \[
   \partial (B \setminus F) = \partial B \cup (B \setminus (B \setminus F))
   \]
   Since \( B \setminus (B \setminus F) = F \):
   \[
   \partial (B \setminus F) = \partial B \cup F
   \]

Therefore, the boundary of the open ball \( B \) minus a finite number of points \( F \) is:
\[
\boxed{\partial B \cup F}

After some research<ul lang="en”*, perhaps this problem is similar to the \"contaminated chessboard\" problem where the vertices are determined by segments and corners. 

Another Idea is to compute the edges as follows: the edges of the polygon can be found by looking at maximal line segments that are aligned with the contaminated cells,hence the vertices are the endpoints of these segments. 

 The polygon must be convex, so these segments form a convex chain.

Thus the algorithm might go:

- Replacing the contaminated cells into a graph of edges.

- Find all line segments (straight lines) that li in a straight line of contaminated cells. Eac线段 represents an edge of the polygon. 

- The vertices are the endpoints of these line segments.

But perhaps more >

Wait, but each edge of the polygon 是 a straight line段 between two vertices. These edge's must account所有的 contaminated cells as lying on their walls. 

Thus, the contaminated cells must lie along the borderline of the polygon's edges。 

Hence, each contaminated cell must be adj a edge of the polygon. Therefore, the contaminated cells 'form the edge' of the polygon.

Thus, each edge of the polygon must form a straight line of contaminated cells. 

 So the edges of the polygon can be found by determining the straight lines that pass through sequences of contaminated cellular. 

The vertices are the endpoints of these straight edges. 

The polygon is formed by connecting these vertices in order, ensuring convexity. 

 The convex hull of the endpoints of these line segments would form the polygon. 

Therefore, the process would be:

- Group the contaminated cells into maximal line segments. 

- The endpoints of these line segments are candidates for vertices. 

- The convex hull of these endpoints will f the polygon, since it's convex. 

Thus, this would give the correct vertices.

 But how to implement this efficiently? 

Grouping the contaminated cells into maximal line segments of contaminated cells is the crux of the problem. 

First, how do we detect maximal line segments?

One way to do this is to iterate over all cells, and for each cell, check in various directions for existing contaminated cells to form a straight line. 

But this could be slow for large M. 

An alternative is to representation the contaminated cells as points and use a line segment detection algorithms, presently the cells are on the lattice grid & and the lines can be in any direction.

Here's a potential approach:

- Process all the contaminated cells, and for each>记录 their coordinates as a ~ set or grid array.

- Iterate through all pairs of contaminated cells and compute the line (slope and intercept) wb是e those two points. 

 But this is O(N squared) which is not feasible for M=2e5. 

 Thus, this is not feasible. 

 So I need a better way. 

 Alternative Idea: the longest maximal line segments can be found by checking in each direction for each cell. 

 For example, for each cell, we can check in 8 directions (up, down, etc.) and see if adjacent cells form straight lines. 

 But this seems complicated. 

 Here's a plan for finding maximal line segments:

1. Represent all contaminated cells in a hash set for quick lookup.

2. For each cell (x,y), check in each possible direction (e.g., the four cardinal directions, diagonal directions), and see how far it extends in that direction. 

3. Identify all maximal line segments. 

However， this might still be time consuming for 2e5 points. 

Another Idea is to represent the contaminated cells in a line as segments..

A line in the form <<ax + by = c>, but estimated the中 quality of lines by grouping cells that fall on the same直线. 

Well, this would involve finding all the distinct lines that the contaminated cells lieOn. For each line, the contaminated cells along it forma straight segment. 

The issue is that with 2e5 points, how to do this efficiently. 

 perhaps using a map that hashes the line equation's parameters. 

 Since the lines can be represented by equations that are simplified by their greatest common divisor. 

 For example, a line through two points (x1,y1) and (x2,y2) can be represented by dx and dy, where dx = x2-x1, dy = y2-y1. 

 The direction of the line is encoded by the direction vector (dx, dy). 

 B几个 the line方程 canbe expressed as a vector 方向 & a point. Thus, for all points along the line, the vector from any selected point must be a multiple of (dx, dy). 

 This requires simplifying the (dx, dy) vector to its smallest terms (divide by their GCD). 

 Thus， for each line segment passing through two or more points, its unique identifier can be the normalized/ direction vector and a base point. 

 But this can be done with the following:

For each contaminated cell (x,y):

- Iterate over all other contaminated cells (other x2,y2), 
-B compute dx= x2-x, dy y2 -y, 
- Compute the GCD of wc尼克的 absolute values of DX and dy. 
- Normalize dx and dy by dividing by the GCD, and en the direction vector as a pair (dx, dy). 
- then, the line can be р stored as a canonical form using this direction vector plus a point. 

 However, doing this for every pair is still O(M^2), which is too slow. 

 Thus, this approach isn't feasible. 

 An alternative approach is needed.

Perhaps we can process each cell and for a few specific directions. 

 For example, for each contam point (x,y), we can check the length of the line seg- in the four cardinal directions, but that won't capture diagonal lines. 

 This might not work.

 

Alternatively, this problem might require an algorithm that can find convex polygon vertices based on the given contaminated cells' coordinate。

Here's the final Idea:

- The vertices of the convex polygon must form a convex hull, and each contaminated cell must be adjacent to exactly 1 or 2 edges of the polygon.

- Thus, the vertices can be found by finding the 端点 of the maximal convex chain that forms the<polygon.

- The最も important point is to find the convex hull tunnel。 

However, this hasn't worked with the sample input.

 Wait in the second sample, the supplied contaminated cells form a larger convex hull than the desired polygon, so this is not the case. 

So perhaps the vertices are the convex hull of certain edges's endpoints, but how?

Perhaps the vertices are the set of points where only two edges meet, and their corson的 contamination values reduce to two, forming the convex hull of the edges' endpoints.

Another observation from the sample:

The first sample output has vertices: (2,3), (2,4), (6,6), (5,2). 

These form a convex polygon. The edges are between each consecutive vertex henew המ.

For each edge, the line petween the two vertices forms a straight line of contaminated cells. 

 Thus, the contaminated cells along each of these lines form the edges of the polygon. 

Thus, the vertices are the endpoints of the longest line segments of contaminated cells. 

 Thus the algorithm can be stated as:

- Find all maximal line segments (in any direction) made of contaminated cells.

    14	from sepal_ui.scripts import utils as su<#sep#>import torch
import torch.nn as nn
from torch.nn import Parameter
import torch.nn.functional as F


def fill_with_neg_inf(t):
    """FP16-compatible function that fills a tensor with -inf."""
    return t.float().fill_(float('-inf')).type_as(t)


def _get_full_incremental_state_key(module_instance, key):
    module_name = module_instance.__class__.__name__
    if not hasattr(module_instance, '_fairseq_instance_id'):
        INCREMENTAL_STATE_INSTANCE_ID[module_name] += 1
        module_instance._fairseq_instance_id = INCREMENTAL_STATE_INSTANCE_ID[
            module_name]
    return '{}.{}.{}'.format(module_name, module_instance.
        _fairseq_instance_id, key)


def get_incremental_state(module, incremental_state, key):
    """Helper for getting incremental state for an nn.Module."""
    full_key = _get_full_incremental_state_key(module, key)
    if incremental_state is None or full_key not in incremental_state:
        return None
    return incremental_state[full_key]


def set_incremental_state(module, incremental_state, key, value):
    """Helper for setting incremental state for an nn.Module."""
    if incremental_state is not None:
        full_key = _get_full_incremental_state_key(module, key)
        incremental_state[full_key] = value


class MultiheadAttention(nn.Module):
    """Multi-headed attention.
    See "Attention Is All You Need" for more details.
    """

    def __init__(self, embed_dim, num_heads, dropout=0.0, bias=True):
        super().__init__()
        self.embed_dim = embed_dim
        self.num_heads = num_heads
        self.dropout = dropout
        self.head_dim = embed_dim // num_heads
        assert self.head_dim * num_heads == self.embed_dim, 'embed_dim must be divisible by num_heads'
        self.scaling = self.head_dim ** -0.5
        self._mask = None
        self.in_proj_weight = Parameter(torch.Tensor(3 * embed_dim, embed_dim))
        if bias:
            self.in_proj_bias = Parameter(torch.Tensor(3 * embed_dim))
        else:
            self.register_parameter('in_proj_bias', None)
        self.out_proj = nn.Linear(embed_dim, embed_dim, bias=bias)
        self.reset_parameters()

    def reset_parameters(self):
        nn.init.xavier_uniform_(self.in_proj_weight)
        nn.init.xavier_uniform_(self.out_proj.weight)
        if self.in_proj_bias is not None:
            nn.init.constant_(self.in_proj_bias, 0.0)
            nn.init.constant_(self.out_proj.bias, 0.0)

    def forward(self, query, key, value, mask_future_timesteps=False,
        key_padding_mask=None, incremental_state=None, need_weights=True,
        static_kv=False):
        """Input shape: Time x Batch x Channel
        Self-attention can be implemented by passing in the same arguments for
        query, key and value. Future timesteps can be masked with the
        `mask_future_timesteps` argument. Padding elements can be excluded from
        the key by passing a binary ByteTensor (`key_padding_mask`) with shape:
        batch x src_len, where padding elements are indicated by 1s.
        """
        qkv_same = query.data_ptr() == key.data_ptr() == value.data_ptr()
        kv_same = key.data_ptr() == value.data_ptr()
        tgt_len, bsz, embed_dim = query.size()
        assert embed_dim == self.embed_dim
        assert list(query.size()) == [tgt_len, bsz, embed_dim]
        assert key.size() == value.size()
        if incremental_state is not None:
            saved_state = self._get_input_buffer(incremental_state)
            if 'prev_key' in saved_state:
                if static_kv:
                    assert kv_same and not qkv_same
                    key = value = None
        else:
            saved_state = None
        if qkv_same:
            q, k, v = self.in_proj_qkv(query)
        elif kv_same:
            q = self.in_proj_q(query)
            if key is None:
                assert value is None
                k = v = q.new(0)
            else:
                k, v = self.in_proj_kv(key)
        else:
            q = self.in_proj_q(query)
            k = self.in_proj_k(key)
            v = self.in_proj_v(value)
        q *= self.scaling
        if saved_state is not None:
            if 'prev_key' in saved_state:
                k = torch.cat((saved_state['prev_key'], k), dim=0)
            if 'prev_value' in saved_state:
                v = torch.cat((saved_state['prev_value'], v), dim=0)
            saved_state['prev_key'] = k
            saved_state['prev_value'] = v
            self._set_input_buffer(incremental_state, saved_state)
        src_len = k.size(0)
        if key_padding_mask is not None:
            assert key_padding_mask.size(0) == bsz
            assert key_padding_mask.size(1) == src_len
        q = q.contiguous().view(tgt_len, bsz * self.num_heads, self.head_dim
            ).transpose(0, 1)
        k = k.contiguous().view(src_len, bsz * self.num_heads, self.head_dim
            ).transpose(0, 1)
        v = v.contiguous().view(src_len, bsz * self.num_heads, self.head_dim
            ).transpose(0, 1)
        attn_weights = torch.bmm(q, k.transpose(1, 2))
        assert list(attn_weights.size()) == [bsz * self.num_heads, tgt_len,
            src_len]
        if mask_future_timesteps and incremental_state is None:
            assert query.size() == key.size(
                ), 'mask_future_timesteps only applies to self-attention'
            attn_weights += self.buffered_mask(attn_weights).unsqueeze(0)
        if key_padding_mask is not None:
            attn_weights = attn_weights.view(bsz, self.num_heads, tgt_len,
                src_len)
            attn_weights = attn_weights.float().masked_fill(key_padding_mask
                .unsqueeze(1).unsqueeze(2), float('-inf')).type_as(attn_weights
                )
            attn_weights = attn_weights.view(bsz * self.num_heads, tgt_len,
                src_len)
        attn_weights = F.softmax(attn_weights.float(), dim=-1).type_as(
            attn_weights)
        attn_weights = F.dropout(attn_weights, p=self.dropout, training=
            self.training)
        attn = torch.bmm(attn_weights, v)
        assert list(attn.size()) == [bsz * self.num_heads, tgt_len, self.
            head_dim]
        attn = attn.transpose(0, 1).contiguous().view(tgt_len, bsz, embed_dim)
        attn = self.out_proj(attn)
        attn_weights = attn_weights.view(bsz, self.num_heads, tgt_len, src_len)
        attn_weights = attn_weights.sum(dim=1) / self.num_heads
        return attn, attn_weights

    def in_proj_qkv(self, query):
        return self._in_proj(query).chunk(3, dim=-1)

    def in_proj_kv(self, key):
        return self._in_proj(key, start=self.embed_dim).chunk(2, dim=-1)

    def in_proj_q(self, query):
        return self._in_proj(query, end=self.embed_dim)

    def in_proj_k(self, key):
        return self._in_proj(key, start=self.embed_dim, end=2 * self.embed_dim)

    def in_proj_v(self, value):
        return self._in_proj(value, start=2 * self.embed_dim)

    def _in_proj(self, input, start=None, end=None):
        weight = self.in_proj_weight
        bias = self.in_proj_bias
        if end is not None:
            weight = weight[:end, :]
            if bias is not None:
                bias = bias[:end]
        if start is not None:
            weight = weight[start:, :]
            if bias is not None:
                bias = bias[start:]
        return F.linear(input, weight, bias)

    def buffered_mask(self, tensor):
        dim = tensor.size(-1)
        if self._mask is None:
            self._mask = torch.triu(fill_with_neg_inf(tensor.new(dim, dim)), 1)
        if self._mask.size(0) < dim:
            self._mask = torch.triu(fill_with_neg_inf(self._mask.resize_(
                dim, dim)), 1)
        return self._mask[:dim, :dim]

    def reorder_incremental_state(self, incremental_state, new_order):
        """Reorder buffered internal state (for incremental generation)."""
        input_buffer = self._get_input_buffer(incremental_state)
        if input_buffer is not None:
            for k in input_buffer.keys():
                input_buffer[k] = input_buffer[k].index_select(1, new_order)

            self._mask = torch.triu(fill_with_neg_inf(self._mask.resize_(
                dim, dim)), 1)

    def reorder_incremental_state(self, incremental_state, new_order):
        """Reorder buffered internal state (for incremental generation)."""
        input_buffer = self._get_input_buffer(incremental_state)
        if input_buffer is not None:
            for k in input_buffer.keys():
                input_buffer[k] = input_buffer[k].index_select(1, new_order)
            self._set_input_buffer(incremental_state, input_buffer)

    def _get_input_buffer(self, incremental_state):
        return get_incremental_state(self, incremental_state, 'attn_state'
            ) or {}

    def _set_input_buffer(self, incremental_state, buffer):
        set_incremental_state(self, incremental_state, 'attn_state', buffer)

    15	class Divider(v.Divider, SepalWidget):
    16	    """
    17	    A custom Divider with the ability to dynamically change color
    18	    Whenever the type\_ trait is modified, the divider class will change accordingly
    19	
    20	    Args:
    21	        class\_ (str, optional): the initial color of the divider
    22	        kwargs (optional): any parameter from a v.Divider. if set, 'class\_' will be overwritten.
    23	    """
    24	
    25	    type_ = Unicode("").tag(sync=True)
    26	    "str: Added type\_ trait to specify the current color of the divider"
    27	
    28	    def __init__(self, class_="", **kwargs):
    29	        kwargs["class_"] = class_
    30	        super().__init__(**kwargs)
    31	
    32	    @observe("type_")
    33	    def add_class_type(self, change):
    34	        """
    35	        Change the color of the divider according to the type\_
    36	        It is binded to the type\_ traitlet but can also be called manually
    37	
    38	        Args:
    39	            change (dict): the only useful key is 'new' which is the new required color
    40	
    41	        Return:
    42	            self
    43	        """
    44	
    45	        self.class_list.remove(*TYPES)
    46	        self.class_list.add(change["new"])
    47

<#sep#>import math
import torch
import torch as th
from torch.nn import Parameter


def functional_linear3d(input, weight, bias=None):
    """
    Apply a linear transformation to the incoming data: :math:`y = xA^T + b`.

    Shape:
        - Input: :math:`(N, *, in\\_features)` where `*` means any number of
          additional dimensions
        - Weight: :math:`(out\\_features, in\\_features)`
        - Bias: :math:`(out\\_features)`
        - Output: :math:`(N, *, out\\_features)`
    """
    output = input.transpose(0, 1).matmul(weight)
    if bias is not None:
        output += bias.unsqueeze(1)
    return output.transpose(0, 1)


class Linear3D(th.nn.Module):
    """Applies a linear transformation to the incoming data: :math:`y = Ax + b`.

    Args:
        in_features: size of each input sample
        out_features: size of each output sample
        bias: If set to False, the layer will not learn an additive bias.
            Default: ``True``

    Shape:
        - Input: :math:`(N, *, in\\_features)` where :math:`*` means any number of
          additional dimensions
        - Output: :math:`(N, *, out\\_features)` where all but the last dimension
          are the same shape as the input.

    Attributes:
        weight: the learnable weights of the module of shape
            `(out_features x in_features)`
        bias:   the learnable bias of the module of shape `(out_features)`

    Examples::

        >>> m = nn.Linear(3, 20, 30)
        >>> input = torch.randn(128, 20)
        >>> output = m(input)
        >>> print(output.size())
    """

    def __init__(self, channels, in_features, out_features, batch_size=-1,
        bias=True, noise=False):
        super(Linear3D, self).__init__()
        self.in_features = in_features
        self.out_features = out_features
        self.channels = channels
        if noise:
            self.in_features += 1
        self.weight = Parameter(th.Tensor(channels, self.in_features,
            out_features))
        if bias:
            self.bias = Parameter(th.Tensor(channels, out_features))
        else:
            self.register_parameter('bias', None)
        if noise:
            self.register_buffer('noise', th.Tensor(batch_size, channels, 1))
        self.reset_parameters()

    def reset_parameters(self):
        stdv = 1.0 / math.sqrt(self.weight.size(1))
        self.weight.data.uniform_(-stdv, stdv)
        if self.bias is not None:
            self.bias.data.uniform_(-stdv, stdv)

    def forward(self, input, adj_matrix=None, permutation_matrix=None):
        input_ = [input]
        if input.dim() == 2:
            if permutation_matrix is not None:
                input_.append(input.unsqueeze(1).expand([input.shape[0],
                    self.channels, permutation_matrix.shape[1]]))
            elif hasattr(self, 'noise'):
                input_.append(input.unsqueeze(1).expand([input.shape[0],
                    self.channels, self.in_features - 1]))
            else:
                input_.append(input.unsqueeze(1).expand([input.shape[0],
                    self.channels, self.in_features]))
        if adj_matrix is not None and permutation_matrix is not None:
            input_.append((input_[-1].transpose(0, 1) @ (adj_matrix.t().
                unsqueeze(2) * permutation_matrix)).transpose(0, 1))
        elif adj_matrix is not None:
            input_.append(input_[-1] * adj_matrix.t().unsqueeze(0))
        elif permutation_matrix is not None:
            input_.append((input_[-1].transpose(0, 1) @ permutation_matrix).t()
                )
        if hasattr(self, 'noise'):
            self.noise.normal_()
            input_.append(th.cat([input_[-1], self.noise], 2))
        return functional_linear3d(input_[-1], self.weight, self.bias)

    def extra_repr(self):
        return 'in_features={}, out_features={}, bias={}'.format(self.
            in_features, self.out_features, self.bias is not None)

    def apply_filter(self, permutation_matrix):
        transpose_weight = self.weight.transpose(1, 2) @ permutation_matrix
        self.weight = Parameter(transpose_weight.transpose(1, 2))


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'channels': 4, 'in_features': 4, 'out_features': 4}]
<#sep#>Let \( d \) be a function from the set of differentiable functions from \( \mathbb{R} \) to \( \mathbb{R} \) to the set of functions from \( \mathbb{R} \) to \( \mathbb{R} \) such that for any differentiable functions \( f \) and \( g \) from \( \mathbb{R} \) to \( \mathbb{R} \), the following hold:
- \( d(f+g) = d(f) + d(g) \) (sum rule)
- \( d(f*g) = f*d(g) + g*d(f) \) (product rule)
- \( d(f \circ g) = (d(f) \circ g) * d(g) \) (chain rule)

What are the possible functions \( d \) can be? Two obvious examples are \( d(f) = f' \) and \( d(f) = 0 \). Are there any other possibilities?

Okay, so I have this problem here where I need to figure out all possible functions \( d \) that satisfy the sum rule, product rule, and chain rule for differentiable functions from \( \mathbb{R} \) to \( \mathbb{R} \). The question mentions that the obvious answers are the derivative \( d(f) = f' \) and the zero function \( d(f) = 0 \), but are there any others? Hmm, let's try to think through this step by step.

First, let me recall what each of these rules means. The sum rule says that if I take \( d \) of the sum of two functions, it's the same as the sum of \( d \) applied to each function individually. The product rule is similar but for multiplication: \( d(f*g) = f*d(g) + g*d(f) \). The chain rule is about composing functions: \( d(f \circ g) = (d(f) \circ g) * d(g) \).

Now, these rules are exactly the properties that the standard derivative operator satisfies. So, the derivative operator is definitely one such \( d \). The zero operator also trivially satisfies these rules because if you plug in zero on both sides of each equation, you get 0 = 0 + 0, 0 = 0*0 + 0*0, and 0 = 0*0, which all hold true. So those two are obvious examples.

But the question is asking if there are any other possible functions \( d \) besides these two. To check this, maybe I can consider what properties \( d \) must satisfy. Let me try to analyze each rule.

Starting with the sum rule: \( d(f + g) = d(f) + d(g) \). This suggests that \( d \) is linear. Wait, but the standard derivative is linear, and so is the zero operator. If \( d \) is linear, then perhaps any linear operator that satisfies the product and chain rules might work. But linearity alone isn't enough because we also need the product and chain rules to hold.

Let me consider the product rule: \( d(f*g) = f*d(g) + g*d(f) \). This is the Leibniz rule, which is characteristic of derivatives. The chain rule is about composition, so that's another key property of derivatives.

If \( d \) is a derivation (a linear operator satisfying the product rule), then under certain conditions, derivations on the space of smooth functions are multiples of the standard derivative. But in this problem, we're dealing with real-valued functions, and the standard derivative is the only non-trivial derivation there, up to scalar multiples? Suppose we have \( d(f) = c f' \) where \( c \) is a constant. Let's see if that works. Let's check the product rule:

\( d(f*g) = c (f*g)' = c (f'*g + f*g') \).

On the other hand, \( f*d(g) + g*d(f) = f*(c g') + g*(c f') = c (f g' + g f') \).

So that works. Similarly, the sum rule: \( d(f + g) = c (f + g)' = c f' + c g' = d(f) + d(g) \). So that's linear. What about the chain rule?

\( d(f \circ g) = c (f \circ g)' = c (f' \circ g) * g' \).

On the other hand, \( (d(f) \circ g) * d(g) = (c f' \circ g) * (c g') = c^2 (f' \circ g) g' \).

Comparing these, unless \( c = 0 \) or \( c = 1 \), this doesn't hold. So if we take \( c \) different from 0 or 1, the chain rule is not satisfied. So scaling the derivative by a constant other than 1 or 0 breaks the chain rule. Therefore, only \( c = 1 \) (the standard derivative) and \( c = 0 \) (the zero function) would satisfy all three rules.

Therefore, the only possible linear combination would be with \( b = 0 \), so \( d(f) = a f' \). But as we saw earlier, the chain rule requires \( a = 1 \).

Alternatively, maybe a nonlinear operator? But the sum rule requires linearity. Wait, the sum rule is \( d(f + g) = d(f) + d(g) \), which is additivity. If we also have homogeneity, then it's linear. But maybe without assuming continuity, there could be pathological solutions. However, in the problem statement, the functions are differentiable, but the operator \( d \) is from differentiable functions to functions (not necessarily differentiable). But the problem doesn't specify any continuity or other conditions, so maybe there are more exotic solutions?

Wait, but even in the case of derivations on rings, over the real numbers, the only derivations on the ring of smooth functions are the standard derivatives (up to some exceptions, but in one variable, it's unique). import torch
from torch.nn import Parameter
import torch.utils.data


def uniform(size, tensor):
    bound = 1.0 / math.sqrt(size)
    if tensor is not None:
        tensor.data.uniform_(-bound, bound)


class DenseGraphConv(torch.nn.Module):
    """See :class:`torch_geometric.nn.conv.GraphConv`.
    """

    def __init__(self, in_channels, out_channels, aggr='add', bias=True):
        assert aggr in ['add', 'mean', 'max']
        super(DenseGraphConv, self).__init__()
        self.in_channels = in_channels
        self.out_channels = out_channels
        self.aggr = aggr
        self.weight = Parameter(torch.Tensor(in_channels, out_channels))
        self.lin = torch.nn.Linear(in_channels, out_channels, bias=bias)
        self.reset_parameters()

    def reset_parameters(self):
        uniform(self.in_channels, self.weight)
        self.lin.reset_parameters()

    def forward(self, x, adj, mask=None):
        """
        Args:
            x (Tensor): Node feature tensor :math:`\\mathbf{X} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{B
                \\times N \\times F}`, with batch-size :math:`B`, (maximum)
                number of nodes :math:`N` for each graph, and feature
                dimension :math:`F`.
            adj (Tensor): Adjacency tensor :math:`\\mathbf{A} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{B
                \\times N \\times N}`. The adjacency tensor is broadcastable in
                the batch dimension, resulting in a shared adjacency matrix for
                the complete batch.
            mask (BoolTensor, optional): Mask matrix
                :math:`\\mathbf{M} \\in {\\{ 0, 1 \\}}^{B \\times N}` indicating
                the valid nodes for each graph. (default: :obj:`None`)
        """
        x = x.unsqueeze(0) if x.dim() == 2 else x
        adj = adj.unsqueeze(0) if adj.dim() == 2 else adj
        B, N, _ = adj.size()
        out = torch.matmul(adj, x)
        out = torch.matmul(out, self.weight)
        if self.aggr == 'mean':
            out = out / adj.sum(dim=-1, keepdim=True).clamp(min=1)
        elif self.aggr == 'max':
            out = out.max(dim=-1)[0]
        out = out + self.lin(x)
        if mask is not None:
            out = out * mask.view(B, N, 1)
        return out

    def __repr__(self):
        return '{}({}, {})'.format(self.__class__.__name__, self.
            in_channels, self.out_channels)


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'in_channels': 4, 'out_channels': 4}]
<#sep#>niz rule. So maybe if we relax linearity, but here the sum rule is additivity. So additivity plus Leibniz rule and chain rule. Hmm.

Alternatively, let's suppose that \( d \) is additive and satisfies the product and chain rules. Let me see if there's a way to construct such a \( d \).

First, consider the case where \( d \) acts on the identity function. Let \( \text{id}(x) = x \). Let's let \( d(\text{id}) = h \), where \( h \) is some function. Then, for any differentiable function \( f \), can we express \( d(f) \) in terms of \( h \) and \( f' \)?

Suppose \( f \) is a polynomial. Let's test with \( f(x) = x^n \). Let's compute \( d(f) \) using the product rule. For example, \( f(x) = x^2 = x * x \). Then,

\( d(x^2) = x * d(x) + d(x) * x = x h(x) + h(x) x = 2 x h(x) \).

But in the standard derivative, \( d/dx(x^2) = 2x \), so if \( h(x) = 1 \), then we get 2x. If \( h(x) = 0 \), we get 0. If \( h(x) \) is something else, like a function, then \( d(x^2) = 2x h(x) \). But then, we also have the chain rule to consider.

Wait, let's use the chain rule. For \( f(x) = x^2 \), we can write it as \( f = g \circ g \), where \( g(x) = x^2 \). Hmm, but that might not be helpful. Alternatively, write \( x^2 \) as the composition of \( f(u) = u^2 \) and \( u(x) = x \). Then,

\( d(f \circ u) = (d(f) \circ u) * d(u) \).

So, \( d(x^2) = d(f \circ u) = (d(f) \circ u) * d(u) \). But \( f(u) = u^2 \), so using the product rule, \( d(f) = 2u * d(u) \). Wait, but we need to express \( d(f) \) in terms of \( u \). Wait, this might be getting too convoluted. Let me think again.

Alternatively, suppose we take \( f(x) = x \). Then, the chain rule applied to \( f \circ f = f \circ f = x \). Wait, that's not helpful. Let's instead take a general function \( f \) and a general \( g \).

Alternatively, consider the function \( f(x) = c \), a constant function. Let's see what \( d \) does to constant functions. Let \( c(x) = c \) for all \( x \).

Using the product rule, take \( c*f \). Then, \( d(c*f) = c*d(f) + f*d(c) \).

But if \( c \) is a constant function, then \( c*f = c \cdot f \), so the standard derivative would give \( c*f' + f*0 = c f' \). However, if \( d(c) \) is not zero, then we have an extra term. Let's see:

If \( d(c) \) is not zero, then \( d(c*f) = c d(f) + f d(c) \). But if \( d \) must satisfy linearity, then \( d(c*f) = c d(f) \), since scalar multiplication (by constant functions) should be linear. Wait, but the problem states that the functions are from \( \mathbb{R} \) to \( \mathbb{R} \), and the set of differentiable functions. The constants are considered as constant functions here. So, in the product rule, when multiplying by a constant function, it should satisfy \( d(c*f) = c*d(f) + f*d(c) \).

But if \( c \) is a constant function, and if we want \( d(c*f) = c*d(f) \), then that would require \( f*d(c) = 0 \) for all \( f \), which implies that \( d(c) = 0 \). Therefore, \( d \) must annihilate constant functions. So \( d(c) = 0 \) for any constant function \( c \).

But in the standard derivative, constants are annihilated. Similarly, the zero operator also sends constants to zero. So this seems necessary.

Therefore, if \( d(c) = 0 \) for all constant functions \( c \), then when we have a function \( f \), we can write \( f(x) = f(a) + \int_a^x f'(t) dt \), but maybe that's complicating things. Let's think in terms of coordinates.

Suppose we define \( d(f) = k(x) f'(x) \), where \( k(x) \) is some fixed function. Let's check whether this satisfies the three rules.

Sum rule: \( d(f + g) = k (f + g)' = k f' + k g' = d(f) + d(g) \). So the sum rule is satisfied regardless of \( k(x) \).

Product rule: \( d(f g) = k (f g)' = k (f' g + f g') \). On the other hand, \( f d(g) + g d(f) = f (k g') + g (k f') = k (f g' + g f') \). So that's the same as \( k (f' g + f g') \). Therefore, the product rule holds for any \( k(x) \).

Chain rule: \( d(f \circ g) = k (f \circ g)' = k (f' \circ g) g' \). On the other hand, \( (d(f) \circ g) * d(g) = (k (f' \circ g)) * (k g') = k^2 (f' \circ g) g' \).

Therefore, for the chain rule to hold, we must have \( k (f' \circ g) g' = k^2 (f' \circ g) g' \). Since this has to hold for all functions \( f \) and \( g \), the coefficients must be equal. So unless \( k = 0 \) or \( k = 1 \), this equality would fail. For example, if \( k \) is a constant function, then \( k = 0 \) or \( k = 1 \). However, if \( k \) is a non-constant function, then the equation \( k(x) = k(g(x)) k(x) \) must hold for all \( x \) and all differentiable functions \( g \).

Wait, let's look at the chain rule again. Let me write it out more carefully. Suppose \( d(f) = k(x) f'(x) \). Then:

Left side: \( d(f \circ g)(x) = k(x) (f \circ g)'(x) = k(x) f'(g(x)) g'(x) \).

Right side: \( (d(f) \circ g)(x) * d(g)(x) = [k(g(x)) f'(g(x))] * [k(x) g'(x)] = k(g(x)) k(x) f'(g(x)) g'(x) \).

Comparing the two sides:

\( k(x) f'(g(x)) g'(x) = k(g(x)) k(x) f'(g(x)) g'(x) \).

Assuming \( f'(g(x)) \) and \( g'(x) \) are not zero (which they can be, but since this must hold for all \( f \) and \( g \), we need the coefficients to match for all \( x \)), we can divide both sides by \( f'(g(x)) g'(x) \) (when they are non-zero), leading to:

\( k(x) = k(g(x)) k(x) \).

So for this equality to hold for all \( g \), we must have either \( k(x) = 0 \) for all \( x \), or \( k(g(x)) = 1 \) for all \( g \) and all \( x \).

But \( k(g(x)) = 1 \) for all \( g \) and all \( x \) is impossible unless \( k \) is the constant function 1. Because suppose there exists some \( y \) such that \( k(y) \neq 1 \). Then, choose \( g(x) = y \<#sep#>human: A super computer has been built in the Turtle Academy of Sciences. The computer consists of n·m·k CPUs. The architecture was the paralellepiped of size n × m × k, split into 1 × 1 × 1 cells, each cell contains exactly one CPU. Thus, each CPU can be simultaneously identified as a group of three numbers from the layer number from 1 to n, the line number from 1 to m and the column number from 1 to k.

In the process of the Super Computer's work the CPUs can send each other messages by the famous turtle scheme: CPU (x, y, z) can send messages to CPUs (x + 1, y, z), (x, y + 1, z) and (x, y, z + 1) (of course, if they exist), there is no feedback, that is, CPUs (x + 1, y, z), (x, y + 1, z) and (x, y, z + 1) cannot send messages to CPU (x, y, z).

Over time some CPUs broke down and stopped working. Such CPUs cannot send messages, receive messages or serve as intermediates in transmitting messages. We will say that CPU (a, b, c) controls CPU (d, e, f) , if there is a chain of CPUs (xi, yi, zi), such that (x1 = a, y1 = b, z1 = c), (xp = d, yp = e, zp = f) (here and below p is the length of the chain) and the CPU in the chain with number i (i < p) can send messages to CPU i + 1.

Turtles are quite concerned about the denial-proofness of the system of communication between the remaining CPUs. For that they want to know the number of critical CPUs. A CPU (x, y, z) is critical, if turning it off will disrupt some control, that is, if there are two distinctive from (x, y, z) CPUs: (a, b, c) and (d, e, f), such that (a, b, c) controls (d, e, f) before (x, y, z) is turned off and stopped controlling it after the turning off.
Input

The first line contains three integers n, m and k (1 ≤ n, m, k ≤ 100) — the dimensions of the Super Computer. 

Then n blocks follow, describing the current state of the processes. The blocks correspond to the layers of the Super Computer in the order from 1 to n. Each block consists of m lines, k characters in each — the description of a layer in the format of an m × k table. Thus, the state of the CPU (x, y, z) is corresponded to the z-th character of the y-th line of the block number x. Character "1" corresponds to a working CPU and character "0" corresponds to a malfunctioning one. The blocks are separated by exactly one empty line.

Output

Print a single integer — the number of critical CPUs, that is, such that turning only this CPU off will disrupt some control.

Examples

Input

2 2 3
000
000

111
111


Output

2


Input

3 3 3
111
111
111

111
111
111

111
111
111


Output

19


Input

1 1 10
0101010101


Output

0

Note

In the first sample the whole first layer of CPUs is malfunctional. In the second layer when CPU (2, 1, 2) turns off, it disrupts the control by CPU (2, 1, 3) over CPU (2, 1, 1), and when CPU (2, 2, 2) is turned off, it disrupts the control over CPU (2, 2, 3) by CPU (2, 2, 1).

In the second sample all processors except for the corner ones are critical.

In the third sample there is not a single processor controlling another processor, so the answer is 0.

   362	    def get_timeout(self, text):
   363	        """
   364	        Calculate timeout in miliseconds to read the message
   365	
   366	        Args:
   367	            text (str): the text displayed in the banner to adapt the duration of the timeout
   368	
   369	        Returns
   370	            (int): the duration of the timeout in milliseconds
   371	        """
   372	
   373	        wpm = 180  # readable words per minute
   374	        word_length = 5  # standardized number of chars in calculable word
   375	        words = len(text) / word_length
   376	        words_time = ((words / wpm) * 60) * 1000
   377	
   378	        delay = 1500  # milliseconds before user starts reading the notification
   379	        bonus = 1000  # extra time
   380
