import torch.nn as nn
import torch.nn.functional as F


def reduce_loss(loss, reduction):
    """Reduce loss as specified.

    Args:
        loss (Tensor): Elementwise loss tensor.
        reduction (str): Options are "none", "mean" and "sum".
    Return:
        Tensor: Reduced loss tensor.
    """
    reduction_enum = F._Reduction.get_enum(reduction)
    if reduction_enum == 0:
        return loss
    elif reduction_enum == 1:
        return loss.mean()
    elif reduction_enum == 2:
        return loss.sum()


def weight_reduce_loss(loss, weight=None, reduction='mean', avg_factor=None):
    """Apply element-wise weight and reduce loss.

    Args:
        loss (Tensor): Element-wise loss.
        weight (Tensor): Element-wise weights.
        reduction (str): Same as built-in losses of PyTorch.
        avg_factor (float): Average factor when computing the mean of losses.
    Returns:
        Tensor: Processed loss values.
    """
    if weight is not None:
        loss = loss * weight
    if avg_factor is None:
        loss = reduce_loss(loss, reduction)
    elif reduction == 'mean':
        loss = loss.sum() / avg_factor
    elif reduction != 'none':
        raise ValueError('avg_factor can not be used with reduction="sum"')
    return loss


def asymmetric_loss(pred, target, weight=None, gamma_pos=1.0, gamma_neg=4.0,
    clip=0.05, reduction='mean', avg_factor=None):
    """asymmetric loss.
    Please refer to the `paper <https://arxiv.org/abs/2009.14119>`__ for
    details.
    Args:
        pred (torch.Tensor): The prediction with shape (N, \\*).
        target (torch.Tensor): The ground truth label of the prediction with
            shape (N, \\*).
        weight (torch.Tensor, optional): Sample-wise loss weight with shape
            (N, ). Defaults to None.
        gamma_pos (float): positive focusing parameter. Defaults to 0.0.
        gamma_neg (float): Negative focusing parameter. We usually set
            gamma_neg > gamma_pos. Defaults to 4.0.
        clip (float, optional): Probability margin. Defaults to 0.05.
        reduction (str): The method used to reduce the loss.
            Options are "none", "mean" and "sum". If reduction is 'none' , loss
            is same shape as pred and label. Defaults to 'mean'.
        avg_factor (int, optional): Average factor that is used to average
            the loss. Defaults to None.
    Returns:
        torch.Tensor: Loss.
    """
    assert pred.shape == target.shape, 'pred and target should be in the same shape.'
    eps = 1e-08
    pred_sigmoid = pred.sigmoid()
    target = target.type_as(pred)
    if clip and clip > 0:
        pt = (1 - pred_sigmoid + clip).clamp(max=1) * (1 - target
            ) + pred_sigmoid * target
    else:
        pt = (1 - pred_sigmoid) * (1 - target) + pred_sigmoid * target
    asymmetric_weight = (1 - pt).pow(gamma_pos * target + gamma_neg * (1 -
        target))
    loss = -torch.log(pt.clamp(min=eps)) * asymmetric_weight
    if weight is not None:
        assert weight.dim() == 1
        weight = weight.float()
        if pred.dim() > 1:
            weight = weight.reshape(-1, 1)
    loss = weight_reduce_loss(loss, weight, reduction, avg_factor)
    return loss


class AsymmetricLoss(nn.Module):
    """asymmetric loss.

    Args:
        gamma_pos (float): positive focusing parameter.
            Defaults to 0.0.
        gamma_neg (float): Negative focusing parameter. We
            usually set gamma_neg > gamma_pos. Defaults to 4.0.
        clip (float, optional): Probability margin. Defaults to 0.05.
        reduction (str): The method used to reduce the loss into
            a scalar.
        loss_weight (float): Weight of loss. Defaults to 1.0.
    """

    def __init__(self, gamma_pos=0.0, gamma_neg=4.0, clip=0.05, reduction=
        'mean', loss_weight=1.0):
        super(AsymmetricLoss, self).__init__()
        self.gamma_pos = gamma_pos
        self.gamma_neg = gamma_neg
        self.clip = clip
        self.reduction = reduction
        self.loss_weight = loss_weight

    def forward(self, pred, target, weight=None, avg_factor=None,
        reduction_override=None):
        """asymmetric loss."""
        assert reduction_override in (None, 'none', 'mean', 'sum')
        reduction = (reduction_override if reduction_override else self.
            reduction)
        loss_cls = self.loss_weight * asymmetric_loss(pred, target, weight,
            gamma_pos=self.gamma_pos, gamma_neg=self.gamma_neg, clip=self.
            clip, reduction=reduction, avg_factor=avg_factor)
        return loss_cls


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {}]
<#sep#>3(1 +1 + z) - 2(1*1 +1*z +1*z) = 3(2 + z) - 2(1 + 2z) = 6 +3z -2 -4z =4 - z. So as z increases from 0 to 1, the expression decreases from 4 to 3. Therefore, maximum in this edge is at z=0, giving 4.

Similarly, if we fix one variable at 1 and another at 1, and vary the third, the maximum is at 4 when the third is 0.

Alternatively, what if two variables are at some value between 0 and1 and the third is 1? Let's try x=1, y and z vary. Then f= 3(1 + y + z) -2( y + z + y z )

Hmm, maybe set partial derivatives? But maybe it's easier to see that if we fix x=1, and set y and z, perhaps we can check.

Alternatively, consider symmetric cases where two variables are 1 and one is 0, which we already saw gives 4, which is higher than any internal point. Therefore, the maximum is indeed 4.

But let me check another case where one variable is 1, and the other two are something else. Let's suppose x=1, y and z are variables. Then the function is:

3(1 + y + z) - 2(y + z + y z )

Simplify: 3 + 3y + 3z - 2y -2z - 2yz = 3 + y + z - 2 y z

We can treat this as a function of y and z. To maximize 3 + y + z - 2 y z, with y, z ∈ [0,1].

Take partial derivatives:

df/dy = 1 - 2 z

Set to 0: 1 - 2 z =0 --> z=0.5

Similarly, df/dz =1 - 2 y. Set to 0: y=0.5

So critical point at y=0.5, z=0.5. Then the function is 3 +0.5 +0.5 -2*(0.5*0.5)=3 +1 -0.5=3.5.

Which is 3.5, which is less than 4. So even if we take that case, the maximum is 3.5, which is still less than 4. Hence, the maximum remains 4.

Alternatively, suppose we fix one variable at 0. Let's say z=0. Then the expression becomes 3(x + y) - 2xy. Let me see if this can be higher than 4 when x and y vary between 0 and1.

So f(x,y,0) =3x +3y -2xy. Let's find maximum of this in [0,1]^2.

Take partial derivatives:

df/dx =3 -2y

df/dy=3 -2x

Set to zero:

3 -2y=0 --> y=1.5

But y is at most 1, so maximum occurs at boundary.

Similarly, x=1.5, which is outside. So maximum on the boundary. So check corners:

x=0,y=0: 0

x=1,y=0:3

x=0,y=1:3

x=1,y=1:3*2 -2*1=6 -2=4

So maximum here is 4. So even when z=0, the maximum is 4.

Similarly, if we fix z=1, the expression is 3(x + y +1) -2(xy +x + y) = 3x +3y +3 -2xy -2x -2y = (3x -2x) + (3y -2y) +3 -2xy = x + y +3 -2xy.

So f(x,y,1) =x + y +3 -2xy.

To find maximum over x,y in [0,1].

Again, take partial derivatives:

df/dx =1 -2y

df/dy=1 -2x

Set to zero:

1 -2y=0 --> y=0.5

1 -2x=0 --> x=0.5

Critical point at (0.5,0.5): value is 0.5 +0.5 +3 -2*(0.25)=4 -0.5=3.5

Compare with boundaries:

Corners:

(0,0):0+0+3 -0=3

(1,0):1+0+3 -0=4

(0,1): same as (1,0), 4

(1,1):1 +1 +3 -2*1=5 -2=3

So maximum here is 4. Therefore, when z=1, the maximum is 4 again. So even when we fix z=1, the maximum is 4.

Hence, in all cases, the maximum occurs when two variables are 1 and the third is 0. Hence, the maximum value of the expression is 4.

Therefore, the answer is 4, and it's achieved at points like (1,1,0), (1,0,1), and (0,1,1).

**Final Answer**
The largest value of the expression is \boxed{4}.
</think>To find the largest value of the expression \(3(x + y + z) - 2(xy + xz + yz)\) given \(0 \leq x, y, z \leq 1\), we will evaluate the expression at the boundary points of the cube defined by \([0, 1]^3\).

1. **Evaluate at the vertices of the cube:**

   - **All variables are 1:**
     \[
     3(1 + 1 + 1) - 2(1 \cdot 1 + 1 \cdot 1 + 1 \cdot 1) = 9 - 6 = 3
     \]

   - **Two variables are 1, one is 0:**
     For example, \(x = 1, y = 1, z = 0\):
     \[
     3(1 + 1 + 0) - 2(1 \cdot 1 + 1 \cdot 0 + 1 \cdot 0) = 6 - 2 = 4
     \]

   - **One variable is 1, the others are 0:**
     For example, \(x = 1, y = 0, z = 0\):
     \[
     3(1 + 0 + 0) - 2(0 + 0 + 0) = 3 - 0 = 3
     \]

   - **All variables are 0:**
     \[
     3(0 + 0 + 0) - 2(0 + 0 + 0) = 0
     \]

2. **Check critical points inside the cube:**

   To find critical points, we take the partial derivatives and set them to zero:
   \[
   \frac{\partial}{\partial x} \left(3(x + y + z) - 2(xy + xz + yz)\right) = 3 - 2(y + z) = 0 \implies y + z = \frac{3}{2}
   \]
   \[
   \frac{\partial}{\partial y} \left(3(x + y + z) - 2(xy + xz + yz)\right) = 3 - 2(x + z) = 0 \implies x + z = \frac{3}{2}
   \]
   \[
   \frac{\partial}{\partial z} \left(3(x + y + z) - 2(xy + xz + yz)\right) = 3 - 2(x + y) = 0 \implies x + y = \frac{3}{2}
   \]

   Solving the system of equations:
   \[
   y + z = \frac{3}{2}, \quad x + z = \frac{3}{2}, \quad x + y = \frac{3}{2}
   \]

   Adding all three equations:
   \[
   2(x + y + z) = \frac{9}{2} \implies x + y + z = \frac{9}{4}
   \]

   Substituting \(x + y + z = \frac{9}{4}\) into each equation:
   \[
   y + z = \frac{3}{2} \implies \frac{9}{4} - x<#sep#>human: Suppose we have a matrix like this:

After 1 last minute, fire ends burning the whole terrain.

The challenge

Every cell will have a positive (non-zero) burn time. You cannot assume a maximum value for the cells.
The matrix does not need to be square nor symmetric.
The matrix can be 0-indexed or 1-indexed, as you like.
The position can be given as a single parameter with a tuple of integers, two separate parameters of whatever other reasonable format.
The dimensions of the matrix cannot be specified as input parameters.
You do not need to output every intermediate step, just the amount of time asked. But I won't complain if the steps are visualized in any way.

   364	        Calculate timeout in miliseconds to read the message

   368	
   369	        Returns
   370	            (int): the duration of the timeout in milliseconds
   371	        """
   372	
   373	        wpm = 180  # readable words per minute
   374	        word_length = 5  # standardized number of chars in calculable word
   375	        words = len(text) / word_length
   376	        words_time = ((words / wpm) * 60) * 1000
   377	
   378	        delay = 1500  # milliseconds before user starts reading the notification
   379	        bonus = 1000  # extra time
   380	
   381	        return delay + words_time + bonus

assistant<#sep#>import functools
import torch
import torch.nn as nn
import torch.nn.functional as F


def reduce_loss(loss, reduction):
    """Reduce loss as specified.

    Args:
        loss (Tensor): Elementwise loss tensor.
        reduction (str): Options are "none", "mean" and "sum".

    Return:
        Tensor: Reduced loss tensor.
    """
    reduction_enum = F._Reduction.get_enum(reduction)
    if reduction_enum == 0:
        return loss
    elif reduction_enum == 1:
        return loss.mean()
    elif reduction_enum == 2:
        return loss.sum()


def weight_reduce_loss(loss, weight=None, reduction='mean', avg_factor=None):
    """Apply element-wise weight and reduce loss.

    Args:
        loss (Tensor): Element-wise loss.
        weight (Tensor): Element-wise weights.
        reduction (str): Same as built-in losses of PyTorch.
        avg_factor (float): Avarage factor when computing the mean of losses.

    Returns:
        Tensor: Processed loss values.
    """
    if weight is not None:
        loss = loss * weight
    if avg_factor is None:
        loss = reduce_loss(loss, reduction)
    elif reduction == 'mean':
        loss = loss.sum() / avg_factor
    elif reduction != 'none':
        raise ValueError('avg_factor can not be used with reduction="sum"')
    return loss


def weighted_loss(loss_func):
    """Create a weighted version of a given loss function.

    To use this decorator, the loss function must have the signature like
    `loss_func(pred, target, **kwargs)`. The function only needs to compute
    element-wise loss without any reduction. This decorator will add weight
    and reduction arguments to the function. The decorated function will have
    the signature like `loss_func(pred, target, weight=None, reduction='mean',
    avg_factor=None, **kwargs)`.

    :Example:

    >>> import torch
    >>> @weighted_loss
    >>> def l1_loss(pred, target):
    >>>     return (pred - target).abs()

    >>> pred = torch.Tensor([0, 2, 3])
    >>> target = torch.Tensor([1, 1, 1])
    >>> weight = torch.Tensor([1, 0, 1])

    >>> l1_loss(pred, target)
    tensor(1.3333)
    >>> l1_loss(pred, target, weight)
    tensor(1.)
    >>> l1_loss(pred, target, reduction='none')
    tensor([1., 1., 2.])
    >>> l1_loss(pred, target, weight, avg_factor=2)
    tensor(1.5000)
    """

    @functools.wraps(loss_func)
    def wrapper(pred, target, weight=None, reduction='mean', avg_factor=
        None, **kwargs):
        loss = loss_func(pred, target, **kwargs)
        loss = weight_reduce_loss(loss, weight, reduction, avg_factor)
        return loss
    return wrapper


@weighted_loss
def l1_loss(pred, target):
    assert pred.size() == target.size() and target.numel() > 0
    loss = torch.abs(pred - target)
    return loss


class L1Loss(nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, reduction='mean', loss_weight=1.0):
        super(L1Loss, self).__init__()
        self.reduction = reduction
        self.loss_weight = loss_weight

    def forward(self, pred, target, weight=None, avg_factor=None,
        reduction_override=None):
        assert reduction_override in (None, 'none', 'mean', 'sum')
        reduction = (reduction_override if reduction_override else self.
            reduction)
        loss_bbox = self.loss_weight * l1_loss(pred, target, weight,
            reduction=reduction, avg_factor=avg_factor)
        return loss_bbox


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {}]

   \]

   Adding all three equations:
   \[
   2(x + y + z) = \frac{9}{2} \implies x + y + z = \frac{9}{4}
   \]

   Substituting \(x + y + z = \frac{9}{4}\) into each equation:
   \[
   y + z = \frac{3}{2} \implies \frac{9}{4} - x = \frac{3}{2} \implies x = \frac{3}{4}
   \]
   \[
   x + z = \frac{3}{2} \implies \frac{9}{4} - y = \frac{3}{2} \implies y = \frac{3}{4}
   \]
   \[
   x + y = \frac{3}{2} \implies \frac{9}{4} - z = \frac{3}{2} \implies z = \frac{3}{4}
   \]

   The critical point is \((x, y, z) = \left(\frac{3}{4}, \frac{3}{4}, \frac{3}{4}\right)\). Evaluating the expression at this point:
   \[
   3\left(\frac{3}{4} + \frac{3}{4} + \frac{3}{4}\right) - 2\left(\frac{3}{4} \cdot \frac{3}{4} + \frac{3}{4} \cdot \frac{3}{4} + \frac{3}{4} \cdot \frac{3}{4}\right) = 3 \cdot \frac{9}{4} - 2 \cdot 3 \cdot \left(\frac{9}{16}\right) = \frac{27}{4} - \frac{27}{8} = \frac{27}{8} = 3.375
   \]

3. **Conclusion:**

   The maximum value of the expression is achieved at the boundary points where two variables are 1 and one is 0. Hmm, maybe let's think of it in terms of an event-based system:

We can model it as follows: 

Initially, the start cell has a start time of 0. The priority queue should hold events. The first event is the end of the start cell's burn, which occurs at time 0 + B[start] = T0. 

At that time, all of the neighbors can potentially start burning. For each neighbor, if their current start time hasn't been set yet, their start time can be T0. Then their finish time is T0 + B[neighbor].

These neighbors are then added to the priority queue with their finish time, so that when those finish times come around, their neighbors can then be triggered. 

So this is basically a BFS processed in a priority queue (min-heap) sorted by the finish time?

For each neighbor (nx, ny), the earliest start time for (nx, ny) can be the T of x,y, since once x,y has finished burning at T, then the fire can start spreading into (nx, ny).

The process can be thought of as:

Initially, the queue is a priority heap where the first item is the finish time of the start cell (0 + B[s]). Then, when we process this finish time, we check all adjacent cells.

We then need to schedule their finish time to be processed, so that when they finish, their neighbors can be processed.

Wait here's the process step by step:

We have an array s that tracks the current earliest start time of each cell.

The priority queue (min-heap) stores the events in order of their finish time, so that we process the earliest finish times first. 

The algorithm can be:

Initialize all s to infinity (except start is 0).

We set finish_time of start cell as s_start + B[start] = 0 + B[start].

1. Extract the cell with the smallest finish time (current_ft) and coordinates (x,y).

2. For each of the four neighbors (nx, ny):

   a. Compute the earliest possible start time for (nx, ny). This "earliest possible" is exactly current_ft (since x,y has finished at current_ft).

   b. If s(nx, ny) > current_ft, then we can update s(nx, ny) to current_ft. Then the finish time for (nx, ny) would be current_ft + B[nx][ny]. 

   c. Check if (nx, ny) already has a valid start time (i.e., if this new start time (current_ft) is indeed less than the previous one). 

   d. If so, then record the new finish time (new_ft = current_ft + B[nx][ny]), and add this to the priority queue.

   170	def catch_errors(alert, debug=False):
   171	    """
   172	    Decorator to execute try/except sentence
   173	    and<#sep#>import functools
import torch
import torch.nn as nn
import torch.nn.functional as F


def reduce_loss(loss, reduction):
    """Reduce loss as specified.

    Args:
        loss (Tensor): Elementwise loss tensor.
        reduction (str): Options are "none", "mean" and "sum".

    Return:
        Tensor: Reduced loss tensor.
    """
    reduction_enum = F._Reduction.get_enum(reduction)
    if reduction_enum == 0:
        return loss
    elif reduction_enum == 1:
        return loss.mean()
    elif reduction_enum == 2:
        return loss.sum()


def weight_reduce_loss(loss, weight=None, reduction='mean', avg_factor=None):
    """Apply element-wise weight and reduce loss.

    Args:
        loss (Tensor): Element-wise loss.
        weight (Tensor): Element-wise weights.
        reduction (str): Same as built-in losses of PyTorch.
        avg_factor (float): Avarage factor when computing the mean of losses.

    Returns:
        Tensor: Processed loss values.
    """
    if weight is not None:
        loss = loss * weight
    if avg_factor is None:
        loss = reduce_loss(loss, reduction)
    elif reduction == 'mean':
        loss = loss.sum() / avg_factor
    elif reduction != 'none':
        raise ValueError('avg_factor can not be used with reduction="sum"')
    return loss


def weighted_loss(loss_func):
    """Create a weighted version of a given loss function.

    To use this decorator, the loss function must have the signature like
    `loss_func(pred, target, **kwargs)`. The function only needs to compute
    element-wise loss without any reduction. This decorator will add weight
    and reduction arguments to the function. The decorated function will have
    the signature like `loss_func(pred, target, weight=None, reduction='mean',
    avg_factor=None, **kwargs)`.

    :Example:

    >>> import torch
    >>> @weighted_loss
    >>> def l1_loss(pred, target):
    >>>     return (pred - target).abs()

    >>> pred = torch.Tensor([0, 2, 3])
    >>> target = torch.Tensor([1, 1, 1])
    >>> weight = torch.Tensor([1, 0, 1])

    >>> l1_loss(pred, target)
    tensor(1.3333)
    >>> l1_loss(pred, target, weight)
    tensor(1.)
    >>> l1_loss(pred, target, reduction='none')
    tensor([1., 1., 2.])
    >>> l1_loss(pred, target, weight, avg_factor=2)
    tensor(1.5000)
    """

    @functools.wraps(loss_func)
    def wrapper(pred, target, weight=None, reduction='mean', avg_factor=
        None, **kwargs):
        loss = loss_func(pred, target, **kwargs)
        loss = weight_reduce_loss(loss, weight, reduction, avg_factor)
        return loss
    return wrapper


@weighted_loss
def smooth_l1_loss(pred, target, beta=1.0):
    assert beta > 0
    assert pred.size() == target.size() and target.numel() > 0
    diff = torch.abs(pred - target)
    loss = torch.where(diff < beta, 0.5 * diff * diff / beta, diff - 0.5 * beta
        )
    return loss


class SmoothL1Loss(nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, beta=1.0, reduction='mean', loss_weight=1.0):
        super(SmoothL1Loss, self).__init__()
        self.beta = beta
        self.reduction = reduction
        self.loss_weight = loss_weight

    def forward(self, pred, target, weight=None, avg_factor=None,
        reduction_override=None, **kwargs):
        assert reduction_override in (None, 'none', 'mean', 'sum')
        reduction = (reduction_override if reduction_override else self.
            reduction)
        loss_bbox = self.loss_weight * smooth_l1_loss(pred, target, weight,
            beta=self.beta, reduction=reduction, avg_factor=avg_factor, **
            kwargs)
        return loss_bbox


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {}]

But according to this, the angle KIB is 90 degrees, but the calculation leads to a contradiction<#sep#> but once we process one entry, we can check if the s stored in the queue is still valid. 

Therefore, when removing an event from the priority queue (say with finish time F of cell (a,b)), 

we compare s[a][b] + B[a][b] (the actual finish time) with F. Because when the event was added, maybe another path found a better (smaller) s for cell (a,b), so the current finish time may not be F anymore. 

Thus, if s[a][b] + B[a][b] < F. Then the current event is invalid and can be skipped. 

Otherwise, we process the previous steps.

This way, even if multiple entries are present, only the one with the earliest start is processed. 

So the steps are:

Initialize s as infinity except start (0)

The priority queue (min-heap) has entries (finish_time, x, y). 

Start:

Push (0+B[start_i][start_j], start_i, start_j)

Initialize the result array (or variables) needed.

Then:

While the heap is not empty:

- Pop (current_ft, x, y) from the heap.

- Check if current_ft > s[x][y] + B[x][y]. 

   If yes, then this is an obsolete entry, skip.

- Else, process its neighbors:

   For each direction:

      nx, ny = x + dx, y + dy.

      Check if nx, ny is within the matrix boundaries.

      Compute candidate start time for nx, ny as s_start_candidate = current_ft (the finish time of x,y)

         Because x,y finishing at current_ft allows nx,ny to start then.

      If this candidate is better (smaller) than the current s[nx][ny], then update s[nx][ny] to s_start_candidate and compute new_ft = candidate + B[nx][ny].

      Then add (new_ft, nx, ny) to the priority queue.

This way, even if the cell (nx, ny) had already a recorded start time, but a better path (earlier start) is found, it will be updated and added to the queue. 

Once all events are processed, then the finish time of each cell is s[i][j] + B[i][j]. We just need to find the maximum among all these finish times. 

Wait, but what about cells that can't be reached by fire? Because, all cells are part of the matrix and the fire must burn everything?

No, because the fire must reach every cell. Since the original starting cell is given and each cell must catch fire from an adjacent path. But every grid cell must have a path from the starting cell via adjacent moves (the problem requires to return the time for the entire terrain to be consumed). So it's implied that the terrain is connected and fire can spread to every cell. 

Thus, the algorithm should cover all cells.

Therefore, the maximum finish time among all cells will be the answer. 

So, the algorithm is feasible. Now code-wise, how to implement this.

First, the steps in code:

- get the input matrix, and starting coordinates.

Wait input: The matrix is NxM. Since it can be in any format, but for programming, we can read a list of lists (in Python for example).

Starting pos can be given as (row, column) values (like x,y, or reversed). Also need to account for zero-based or one-based. So in the problem statement, in the first example the starting cell is [2][2], which is zero-based, so the matrix is 5x5. The code can just take the position as given (assuming zero-based).

Now for Dijkstra-like approach with a priority queue.

In Python, we can use a priority queue implemented via heapq. 

First, we need to represent the matrix as B, and then initialize s as a 2D array of size N x M, with s[i][j] = infinity (except start). 

Then steps:

Initialize a min-heap. 

Then the code outline is something like:

import heapq

def compute_time(matrix, start):

    rows = len(matrix)

    cols = len(matrix[0]) if rows else 0

    B = matrix

    start_x, start_y = start

    INF = float('inf')

    s = [[INF for _ in range(cols)] for _ in range(rows)]

    s[start_x][start_y] = 0  # start time of start cell is 0

    # priority queue (heap) entries are (finish_time, x, y)

    heap = []

    heapq.heappush(heap, (B[start_x][start_y], start_x, start_y))

    directions = [(-1,0), (1,0), (0,-1), (0,1)]

    while heap:

        current_ft, x, y = heapq.heappop(heap)

        # check if the event is still valid.

        if s[x][y] + B[x][y] != current_ft:

            continue  # a better path was found, ignore this entry

        # process each neighbor:

        for dx, dy in directions:

            nx = x + dx

            ny = y + dy

            if 0 <= nx < rows and 0 <= ny < cols:

                # compute candidate start time, which is current_ft (when this cell finished burning)

                candidate_start = current_ft

                if candidate_start < s[nx][ny]:

                    # update, since this earlier time can be used

                    s[nx][ny] = candidate_start

                    new_ft = candidate_start + B[nx][ny]

                    heapq.heappush(heap, (new_ft, nx, ny))

    # Once done, compute the maximum finish time, which is s[i][j] + B[i][j]

    max_time = 0

    for i in range(rows):

        for j in range(cols):

            finish_time = s[i][j] + B[i][j]

            if finish_time > max_time:

                max_time = finish_time

    return max_time

Hmm, but wait, there's a problem here. Because when the queue processes the current_ft, and the s[x][y], which was already computed, is correct. 

Yes, because before processing, the code checks if s[x][y] + B[x][y] == current_ft. 

So that ensures that this is still the valid entry. 

Wait, the way we stored the current_ft is the original finish time of (x,y), which was the candidate when that cell was added to the queue. But if another route has caused s[x][y] to be smaller, then the actual finish time would be s[x][y] + B[x][y], which is less than current_ft. 

Therefore, when the entry (current_ft, ...) is popped and the actual finish time is lower, we can ignore. 

This is crucial.

Thus this algorithm should work.

s[2][2] =0, B at (2,2) is 3.

The next element to be processed will be the first 5 (say any of the four).

Then look at its neighbors:

adjacent cells are (0,2), (2,2), (1<#sep#>.iam.gserviceaccount.com"

   170	def catch_errors(alert, debug=False):
   171	    """
   172	    Decorator to execute try/except sentence
   173	    and catch errors in the alert message.
   174	    If debug is True then the error is raised anyway
   175	
   176	    Params:
   177	        alert (sw.Alert): Alert to display errors
   178	        debug (bool): Wether to raise the error or not, default to false
   179	    """
   180	
   181	    def decorator_alert_error(func):
   182	        @wraps(func)
   183	        def wrapper_alert_error(*args, **kwargs):
   184	            value = None
   185	            try:
   186	                value = func(*args, **kwargs)
   187	            except Exception as e:
   188	                alert.add_msg(f"{e}", type_="error")
   189	                if debug:
   190	                    raise e
   191	            return value
   192	
   193	        return wrapper_alert_error
   194	
   195	    return decorator_alert_error

   221	def loading_button(alert=None, button=None, debug=False):
   222	    """
   223	    Decorator to execute try/except sentence and toggle loading button object.
   224	    Designed to work within the Tile object, or any object that have a self.btn and self.alert set.
   225	
   226	    Params:
   227	        button (sw.Btn, optional): Toggled button
   228	        alert (sw.Alert, optional): the alert to display the error message
   229	        debug (bool, optional): wether or not the exception should stop the execution. default to False
   230	    """
   231	
   232	    def decorator_loading(func):
   233	        @wraps(func)
   234	        def wrapper_loading(self, *args, **kwargs):
   235	
   236	            # set btn and alert
   237	            # Change name of variable to assign it again in this scope
   238	            button_ = self.btn if not button else button
   239	            alert_ = self.alert if not alert else alert
   240	
   241	            # Clean previous loaded messages in alert
   242	            alert_.reset()
   243	
   244	            button_.toggle_loading()  # Start loading
   245	            value = None
   246	            try:
   247	                # Catch warnings in the process function
   248	                with warnings.catch_warnings(record=True) as w_list:
   249	                    value = func(self, *args, **kwargs)
   250	
   251	                # Check if there are warnings in the function and append them
   252	                # Use append msg as several warnings could be triggered
   253	                if w_list:
   254	
   255	                    # split the warning list
   256	                    w_list_sepal = [
   257	                        w for w in w_list if isinstance(w.message, SepalWarning)
   258	                    ]
   259	
   260	                    # display the sepal one
   261	                    ms_list = [
   262	                        f"{w.category.__name__}: {w.message.args[0]}"
   263	                        for w in w_list_sepal
   264	                    ]
   265	                    [alert_.append_msg(ms, type_="warning") for ms in ms_list]
   266	
   267	                    # only display them in the console if debug mode
   268	                    if debug:
   269	
   270	                        def custom_showwarning(w):
   271	                            return warnings.showwarning(
   272	                                message=w.message,
   273	                                category=w.category,
   274	                                filename=w.filename,
   275	                                lineno=w.lineno,
   276	                                line=w.line,
   277	                            )
   278	
   279	                        [custom_showwarning(w) for w in w_list]
   280	
   281	            except Exception as e:
   282	                alert_.add_msg(f"{e}", "error")
   283	                if debug:
   284	                    button_.toggle_loading()  # Stop loading button if there is an error
   285	                    raise e
   286	
   287	            button_.toggle_loading()  # Stop loading button
   288	
   289	            return value
   290	
   291	        return wrapper_loading
   292	
   293	    return decorator_loading

   <#sep#>import torch
import torch.nn as nn
import torch.nn.functional as F


def reduce_loss(loss, reduction):
    """Reduce loss as specified.

    Args:
        loss (Tensor): Elementwise loss tensor.
        reduction (str): Options are "none", "mean" and "sum".
    Return:
        Tensor: Reduced loss tensor.
    """
    reduction_enum = F._Reduction.get_enum(reduction)
    if reduction_enum == 0:
        return loss
    elif reduction_enum == 1:
        return loss.mean()
    elif reduction_enum == 2:
        return loss.sum()


def weight_reduce_loss(loss, weight=None, reduction='mean', avg_factor=None):
    """Apply element-wise weight and reduce loss.

    Args:
        loss (Tensor): Element-wise loss.
        weight (Tensor): Element-wise weights.
        reduction (str): Same as built-in losses of PyTorch.
        avg_factor (float): Average factor when computing the mean of losses.
    Returns:
        Tensor: Processed loss values.
    """
    if weight is not None:
        loss = loss * weight
    if avg_factor is None:
        loss = reduce_loss(loss, reduction)
    elif reduction == 'mean':
        loss = loss.sum() / avg_factor
    elif reduction != 'none':
        raise ValueError('avg_factor can not be used with reduction="sum"')
    return loss


def binary_cross_entropy(pred, label, weight=None, reduction='mean',
    avg_factor=None, class_weight=None, pos_weight=None):
    """Calculate the binary CrossEntropy loss with logits.
    Args:
        pred (torch.Tensor): The prediction with shape (N, \\*).
        label (torch.Tensor): The gt label with shape (N, \\*).
        weight (torch.Tensor, optional): Element-wise weight of loss with shape
            (N, ). Defaults to None.
        reduction (str): The method used to reduce the loss.
            Options are "none", "mean" and "sum". If reduction is 'none' , loss
            is same shape as pred and label. Defaults to 'mean'.
        avg_factor (int, optional): Average factor that is used to average
            the loss. Defaults to None.
        class_weight (torch.Tensor, optional): The weight for each class with
            shape (C), C is the number of classes. Default None.
        pos_weight (torch.Tensor, optional): The positive weight for each
            class with shape (C), C is the number of classes. Default None.
    Returns:
        torch.Tensor: The calculated loss
    """
    assert pred.dim() == label.dim()
    if class_weight is not None:
        N = pred.size()[0]
        class_weight = class_weight.repeat(N, 1)
    loss = F.binary_cross_entropy_with_logits(pred, label, weight=
        class_weight, pos_weight=pos_weight, reduction='none')
    if weight is not None:
        assert weight.dim() == 1
        weight = weight.float()
        if pred.dim() > 1:
            weight = weight.reshape(-1, 1)
    loss = weight_reduce_loss(loss, weight=weight, reduction=reduction,
        avg_factor=avg_factor)
    return loss


def cross_entropy(pred, label, weight=None, reduction='mean', avg_factor=
    None, class_weight=None):
    """Calculate the CrossEntropy loss.

    Args:
        pred (torch.Tensor): The prediction with shape (N, C), C is the number
            of classes.
        label (torch.Tensor): The gt label of the prediction.
        weight (torch.Tensor, optional): Sample-wise loss weight.
        reduction (str): The method used to reduce the loss.
        avg_factor (int, optional): Average factor that is used to average
            the loss. Defaults to None.
        class_weight (torch.Tensor, optional): The weight for each class with
            shape (C), C is the number of classes. Default None.
    Returns:
        torch.Tensor: The calculated loss
    """
    loss = F.cross_entropy(pred, label, weight=class_weight, reduction='none')
    if weight is not None:
        weight = weight.float()
    loss = weight_reduce_loss(loss, weight=weight, reduction=reduction,
        avg_factor=avg_factor)
    return loss


def soft_cross_entropy(pred, label, weight=None, reduction='mean',
    class_weight=None, avg_factor=None):
    """Calculate the Soft CrossEntropy loss.

    The label can be float.
    Args:
        pred (torch.Tensor): The prediction with shape (N, C), C is the number
            of classes.
        label (torch.Tensor): The gt label of the prediction with shape (N, C).
            When using "mixup", the label can be float.
        weight (torch.Tensor, optional): Sample-wise loss weight.
        reduction (str): The method used to reduce the loss.
        avg_factor (int, optional): Average factor that is used to average
            the loss. Defaults to None.
        class_weight (torch.Tensor, optional): The weight for each class with
            shape (C), C is the number of classes. Default None.
    Returns:
        torch.Tensor: The calculated loss
    """
    loss = -label * F.log_softmax(pred, dim=-1)
    if class_weight is not None:
        loss *= class_weight
    loss = loss.sum(dim=-1)
    if weight is not None:
        weight = weight.float()
    loss = weight_reduce_loss(loss, weight=weight, reduction=reduction,
        avg_factor=avg_factor)
    return loss


class CrossEntropyLoss(nn.Module):
    """Cross entropy loss.

    Args:
        use_sigmoid (bool): Whether the prediction uses sigmoid
            of softmax. Defaults to False.
        use_soft (bool): Whether to use the soft version of CrossEntropyLoss.
            Defaults to False.
        reduction (str): The method used to reduce the loss.
            Options are "none", "mean" and "sum". Defaults to 'mean'.
        loss_weight (float):  Weight of the loss. Defaults to 1.0.
        class_weight (List[float], optional): The weight for each class with
            shape (C), C is the number of classes. Default None.
        pos_weight (List[float], optional): The positive weight for each
            class with shape (C), C is the number of classes. Only enabled in
            BCE loss when ``use_sigmoid`` is True. Default None.
    """

    def __init__(self, use_sigmoid=False, use_soft=False, reduction='mean',
        loss_weight=1.0, class_weight=None, pos_weight=None):
        super(CrossEntropyLoss, self).__init__()
        self.use_sigmoid = use_sigmoid
        self.use_soft = use_soft
        assert not (self.use_soft and self.use_sigmoid
            ), 'use_sigmoid and use_soft could not be set simultaneously'
        self.reduction = reduction
        self.loss_weight = loss_weight
        self.class_weight = class_weight
        self.pos_weight = pos_weight
        if self.use_sigmoid:
            self.cls_criterion = binary_cross_entropy
        elif self.use_soft:
            self.cls_criterion = soft_cross_entropy
        else:
            self.cls_criterion = cross_entropy

    def forward(self, cls_score, label, weight=None, avg_factor=None,
        reduction_override=None, **kwargs):
        assert reduction_override in (None, 'none', 'mean', 'sum')
        reduction = (reduction_override if reduction_override else self.
            reduction)
        if self.class_weight is not None:
            class_weight = cls_score.new_tensor(self.class_weight)
        else:
            class_weight = None
        if self.pos_weight is not None and self.use_sigmoid:
            pos_weight = cls_score.new_tensor(self.pos_weight)
            kwargs.update({'pos_weight': pos_weight})
        else:
            pos_weight = None
        loss_cls = self.loss_weight * self.cls_criterion(cls_score, label,
            weight, class_weight=class_weight, reduction=reduction,
            avg_factor=avg_factor, **kwargs)
        return loss_cls


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {}]

   357	def switch(*params, debug=True, on_widgets=[], targets=[]):
   358	    """
   359	    Decorator to switch the state of input boolean parameters on class widgets or the
   360	    class itself. If on_widgets is defined, it will switch the state of every widget
   361	    parameter, otherwise it will change the state of the class (self). You can also set
   362	    two decorators on the same function, one could affect the class and other the widgets.
   363	
   364	    Args:
   365	        *params (str): any boolean parameter of a SepalWidget.
   366	        debug (bool): Whether trigger or not an Exception if the decorated function fails.
   367	        on_widgets (list(widget_names,)|optional): List of widget names into the class
   368	        targets (list(bool,)|optional); list of the target value (value taht will be set on switch. default to the inverse of the current state.
   369	
   370	    """
   371	
   372	    def decorator_switch(func):
   373	        @wraps(func)
   374	        def wrapper_switch(self, *args, **kwargs):
   375	
   376	            widgets_len = len(on_widgets)
   377	            targets_len = len(targets)
   378	
   379	            # sanity check on targets and on_widgets
   380	            if widgets_len and targets_len:
   381	                if widgets_len != targets_len:
   382	                    raise IndexError(
   383	                        f'the length of "on_widgets" ({widgets_len}) is different from the length of "targets" ({targets_len})'
   384	                    )
   385	
   386	            # create the list of target values based on the target list
   387	            # or the initial values of the widgets params
   388	            # The first one is taken as reference
   389	            if not targets_len:
   390	                w = getattr(self, on_widgets[0]) if widgets_len else self
   391	                targets_ = [bool(getattr(w, p)) for p in params]
   392	            else:
   393	                targets_ = targets
   394	
   395	            if widgets_len:
   396	
   397	                # Verify that the input elements are strings
   398	                wrong_types = [
   399	                    (w, type(w)) for w in on_widgets if not isinstance(w, str)
   400	                ]
   401	
   402	                if len(wrong_types):
   403	                    errors = [
   404	                        f"Received:{w_type} for widget: {w}."
   405	                        for w, w_type in wrong_types
   406	                    ]
   407	
   408	                    raise TypeError(
   409	                        f"All on_widgets list elements has to be strings. [{' '.join(errors)}]"
   410	                    )
   411	
   412	                missing_widgets = [w for w in on_widgets if not hasattr(self, w)]
   413	
   414	                if missing_widgets:
   415	                    raise Exception(
   416	                        f"The provided {missing_widgets} widget(s) does not exist in the current class"
   417	                    )
   418	
   419	                def w_assign(bool_targets):
   420	
   421	                    params_targets = [
   422	                        (p, bool_targets[i]) for i, p in enumerate(params)
   423	                    ]
   424	
   425	                    for (w_name, p_t) in product(on_widgets, params_targets):
   426	                        param, target = p_t
   427	                        widget = getattr(self, w_name)
   428	                        setattr(widget, param, target)
   429	
   430	            else:
   431	
   432	                def w_assign(bool_targets):
   433	                    for i, p in enumerate(params):
   434	                        setattr(self, p, bool_targets[i])
   435	
   436	            # assgn the parameters to the target inverse
   437	            w_assign([not t for t in targets_])
   438	
   439	            # execute the function and catch errors
   440	            try:
   441	                func(self, *args, **kwargs)
   442	
   443	            except Exception as e:
   444	                if debug:
   445	                    w_assign(targets_)
   446	                    raise e
   447	
   448	            # reassign the parameters to the targets
   449	            w_assign(targets_)
   450	
   451	        return wrapper_switch
   452	
   453	    return decorator_switch
   454

   456	def next_string(string):
   457	    """
   458	    Create a string followed by an underscore and a consecutive number
   459	
   460	    Args:
   461	        string (str): the initial string
   462	
   463	    Returns:
   464	        (str): the incremented string
   465	    """
   466	
   467	    # if the string is already numbered the last digit is separeted from the rest of the string by an "_"
   468	    split = string.split("_")
   469	    end = split[-1]
   470	
   471	    if end.isdigit():
   472	        string = "_".join(split[:-1]) + f"_{int(end)+1}"
   473	    else:
   474	        string += "_1"
   475	
   476	    return string

<#sep#>import functools
import torch
import torch.nn as nn
import torch.nn.functional as F


def reduce_loss(loss, reduction):
    """Reduce loss as specified.

    Args:
        loss (Tensor): Elementwise loss tensor.
        reduction (str): Options are "none", "mean" and "sum".

    Return:
        Tensor: Reduced loss tensor.
    """
    reduction_enum = F._Reduction.get_enum(reduction)
    if reduction_enum == 0:
        return loss
    elif reduction_enum == 1:
        return loss.mean()
    elif reduction_enum == 2:
        return loss.sum()


def weight_reduce_loss(loss, weight=None, reduction='mean', avg_factor=None):
    """Apply element-wise weight and reduce loss.

    Args:
        loss (Tensor): Element-wise loss.
        weight (Tensor): Element-wise weights.
        reduction (str): Same as built-in losses of PyTorch.
        avg_factor (float): Avarage factor when computing the mean of losses.

    Returns:
        Tensor: Processed loss values.
    """
    if weight is not None:
        loss = loss * weight
    if avg_factor is None:
        loss = reduce_loss(loss, reduction)
    elif reduction == 'mean':
        loss = loss.sum() / avg_factor
    elif reduction != 'none':
        raise ValueError('avg_factor can not be used with reduction="sum"')
    return loss


def weighted_loss(loss_func):
    """Create a weighted version of a given loss function.

    To use this decorator, the loss function must have the signature like
    `loss_func(pred, target, **kwargs)`. The function only needs to compute
    element-wise loss without any reduction. This decorator will add weight
    and reduction arguments to the function. The decorated function will have
    the signature like `loss_func(pred, target, weight=None, reduction='mean',
    avg_factor=None, **kwargs)`.

    :Example:

    >>> import torch
    >>> @weighted_loss
    >>> def l1_loss(pred, target):
    >>>     return (pred - target).abs()

    >>> pred = torch.Tensor([0, 2, 3])
    >>> target = torch.Tensor([1, 1, 1])
    >>> weight = torch.Tensor([1, 0, 1])

    >>> l1_loss(pred, target)
    tensor(1.3333)
    >>> l1_loss(pred, target, weight)
    tensor(1.)
    >>> l1_loss(pred, target, reduction='none')
    tensor([1., 1., 2.])
    >>> l1_loss(pred, target, weight, avg_factor=2)
    tensor(1.5000)
    """

    @functools.wraps(loss_func)
    def wrapper(pred, target, weight=None, reduction='mean', avg_factor=
        None, **kwargs):
        loss = loss_func(pred, target, **kwargs)
        loss = weight_reduce_loss(loss, weight, reduction, avg_factor)
        return loss
    return wrapper


@weighted_loss
def gaussian_focal_loss(pred, gaussian_target, alpha=2.0, gamma=4.0):
    eps = 1e-12
    pos_weights = gaussian_target.eq(1)
    neg_weights = (1 - gaussian_target).pow(gamma)
    pos_loss = -(pred + eps).log() * (1 - pred).pow(alpha) * pos_weights
    neg_loss = -(1 - pred + eps).log() * pred.pow(alpha) * neg_weights
    return pos_loss + neg_loss


class GaussianFocalLoss(nn.Module):
    """ GaussianFocalLoss is a variant of focal loss.

    More details can be found in the `paper
    <https://arxiv.org/abs/1808.01244>`_
    Code is modified from `kp_utils.py
    <https://github.com/princeton-vl/CornerNet/blob/master/models/py_utils/kp_utils.py#L152>`_  # noqa: E501
    Please notice that the target in GaussianFocalLoss is a gaussian heatmap,
    not 0/1 binary target.

    Args:
        alpha (float): Power of prediction.
        gamma (float): Power of target for negtive samples.
        reduction (str): Options are "none", "mean" and "sum".
        loss_weight (float): Loss weight of current loss.
    """

    def __init__(self, alpha=2.0, gamma=4.0, reduction='mean', loss_weight=1.0
        ):
        super(GaussianFocalLoss, self).__init__()
        self.alpha = alpha
        self.gamma = gamma
        self.reduction = reduction
        self.loss_weight = loss_weight

    def forward(self, pred, target, weight=None, avg_factor=None,
        reduction_override=None):
        assert reduction_override in (None, 'none', 'mean', 'sum')
        reduction = (reduction_override if reduction_override else self.
            reduction)
        loss_reg = self.loss_weight * gaussian_focal_loss(pred, target,
            weight, alpha=self.alpha, gamma=self.gamma, reduction=reduction,
            avg_factor=avg_factor)
        return loss_reg


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {}]

import heapq
def f(m,s0,s1):
    B = m
    R,C = len(B), len(B[0])
    s = [[float('inf')]*C for _ in range(R)]
    s[s0][s1] = 0

    heapq.heappush(h, (B[s0][s1], s0, s1))
    d=[[1,0],[-1,0],[0,1],[0,-1]]

        for dx,dy in d:

            if 0<=nx<R and 0<=ny<C:

                    heapq.heappush(h, (t+B[nx][ny], nx, ny))
    return max(s[x][y]+B[x][y] for x in range(R) for y in range(C))

def f(m,p):

import torch
import torch.nn as nn
import torch.nn.functional as F


def reduce_loss(loss, reduction):
    """Reduce loss as specified.

    Args:
        loss (Tensor): Elementwise loss tensor.
        reduction (str): Options are "none", "mean" and "sum".

    Return:
        Tensor: Reduced loss tensor.
    """
    reduction_enum = F._Reduction.get_enum(reduction)
    if reduction_enum == 0:
        return loss
    elif reduction_enum == 1:
        return loss.mean()
    elif reduction_enum == 2:
        return loss.sum()


def weight_reduce_loss(loss, weight=None, reduction='mean', avg_factor=None):
    """Apply element-wise weight and reduce loss.

    Args:
        loss (Tensor): Element-wise loss.
        weight (Tensor): Element-wise weights.
        reduction (str): Same as built-in losses of PyTorch.
        avg_factor (float): Avarage factor when computing the mean of losses.

    Returns:
        Tensor: Processed loss values.
    """
    if weight is not None:
        loss = loss * weight
    if avg_factor is None:
        loss = reduce_loss(loss, reduction)
    elif reduction == 'mean':
        loss = loss.sum() / avg_factor
    elif reduction != 'none':
        raise ValueError('avg_factor can not be used with reduction="sum"')
    return loss


def weighted_loss(loss_func):
    """Create a weighted version of a given loss function.

    To use this decorator, the loss function must have the signature like
    `loss_func(pred, target, **kwargs)`. The function only needs to compute
    element-wise loss without any reduction. This decorator will add weight
    and reduction arguments to the function. The decorated function will have
    the signature like `loss_func(pred, target, weight=None, reduction='mean',
    avg_factor=None, **kwargs)`.

    :Example:

    >>> import torch
    >>> @weighted_loss
    >>> def l1_loss(pred, target):
    >>>     return (pred - target).abs()

    >>> pred = torch.Tensor([0, 2, 3])
    >>> target = torch.Tensor([1, 1, 1])
    >>> weight = torch.Tensor([1, 0, 1])

    >>> l1_loss(pred, target)
    tensor(1.3333)
    >>> l1_loss(pred, target, weight)
    tensor(1.)
    >>> l1_loss(pred, target, reduction='none')
    tensor([1., 1., 2.])
    >>> l1_loss(pred, target, weight, avg_factor=2)
    tensor(1.5000)
    """

    @functools.wraps(loss_func)
    def wrapper(pred, target, weight=None, reduction='mean', avg_factor=
        None, **kwargs):
        loss = loss_func(pred, target, **kwargs)
        loss = weight_reduce_loss(loss, weight, reduction, avg_factor)
        return loss
    return wrapper


@weighted_loss
def mse_loss(pred, target):
    return F.mse_loss(pred, target, reduction='none')


class MSELoss(nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, reduction='mean', loss_weight=1.0):
        super().__init__()
        self.reduction = reduction
        self.loss_weight = loss_weight

    def forward(self, pred, target, weight=None, avg_factor=None):
        loss = self.loss_weight * mse_loss(pred, target, weight, reduction=
            self.reduction, avg_factor=avg_factor)
        return loss


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {}]
<#sep#>2 a² s + 2 a s² - s³ - 2 a³ + a² s

Combine like terms:

2 a³ - 2 a³ = 0

2 a² s + a² s = 3 a² s

2 a s² remains

-s³

So total:

3 a² s + 2 a s² - s³ = 0

Factor out s:

s (3 a² + 2 a s - s²) = 0

Since s ≠ 0, we have:

3 a² + 2 a s - s² = 0

Which is a quadratic equation in s:

-s² + 2 a s + 3 a² = 0

Multiply by -1:

s² - 2 a s - 3 a² = 0

Solve for s:

s = [2 a ± sqrt(4 a² + 12 a²)] / 2

= [2 a ± sqrt(16 a²)] / 2

= [2 a ± 4 a] / 2

So s = (2 a + 4 a)/2 = 6a /2 = 3a, or s = (2a -4a)/2 = (-2a)/2 = -a

Since s is a length (sqrt(a² + h²)), it must be positive. Therefore, s = 3a.

Therefore, sqrt(a² + h²) = 3a

Squaring both sides:

a² + h² = 9 a²

Therefore, h² = 8 a² => h = 2 a sqrt(2)

So h = 2√2 a

So now we have the relationship between h and a: h = 2√2 a.

Now, we can compute r = (a h)/(a + s) = (a * 2√2 a)/(a + 3a) = (2√2 a²)/(4a) = (2√2 a)/4 = (√2 a)/2

So r = (√2 a)/2

Thus, coordinates of I are (0, r) = (0, (√2 a)/2)

Coordinates of K are (-a/2, h/2) = (-a/2, (2√2 a)/2) = (-a/2, √2 a)

Point B is at (-a, 0)

Point M is the centroid at (0, h/3) = (0, (2√2 a)/3)

Angle at I between M and B. So angle MIB is the angle at point I between points M, I, B.

Vector IB is B - I = (-a - 0, 0 - (√2 a)/2) = (-a, - (√2 a)/2)

import torch.nn as nn
import torch.nn.functional as F


def _expand_onehot_labels(labels, label_weights, label_channels):
    bin_labels = labels.new_full((labels.size(0), label_channels), 0)
    inds = torch.nonzero((labels >= 0) & (labels < label_channels),
        as_tuple=False).squeeze()
    if inds.numel() > 0:
        bin_labels[inds, labels[inds]] = 1
    bin_label_weights = label_weights.view(-1, 1).expand(label_weights.size
        (0), label_channels)
    return bin_labels, bin_label_weights


class GHMC(nn.Module):
    """GHM Classification Loss.

    Details of the theorem can be viewed in the paper
    "Gradient Harmonized Single-stage Detector".
    https://arxiv.org/abs/1811.05181

    Args:
        bins (int): Number of the unit regions for distribution calculation.
        momentum (float): The parameter for moving average.
        use_sigmoid (bool): Can only be true for BCE based loss now.
        loss_weight (float): The weight of the total GHM-C loss.
    """

    def __init__(self, bins=10, momentum=0, use_sigmoid=True, loss_weight=1.0):
        super(GHMC, self).__init__()
        self.bins = bins
        self.momentum = momentum
        edges = torch.arange(bins + 1).float() / bins
        self.register_buffer('edges', edges)
        self.edges[-1] += 1e-06
        if momentum > 0:
            acc_sum = torch.zeros(bins)
            self.register_buffer('acc_sum', acc_sum)
        self.use_sigmoid = use_sigmoid
        if not self.use_sigmoid:
            raise NotImplementedError
        self.loss_weight = loss_weight

    def forward(self, pred, target, label_weight, *args, **kwargs):
        """Calculate the GHM-C loss.

        Args:
            pred (float tensor of size [batch_num, class_num]):
                The direct prediction of classification fc layer.
            target (float tensor of size [batch_num, class_num]):
                Binary class target for each sample.
            label_weight (float tensor of size [batch_num, class_num]):
                the value is 1 if the sample is valid and 0 if ignored.
        Returns:
            The gradient harmonized loss.
        """
        if pred.dim() != target.dim():
            target, label_weight = _expand_onehot_labels(target,
                label_weight, pred.size(-1))
        target, label_weight = target.float(), label_weight.float()
        edges = self.edges
        mmt = self.momentum
        weights = torch.zeros_like(pred)
        g = torch.abs(pred.sigmoid().detach() - target)
        valid = label_weight > 0
        tot = max(valid.float().sum().item(), 1.0)
        n = 0
        for i in range(self.bins):
            inds = (g >= edges[i]) & (g < edges[i + 1]) & valid
            num_in_bin = inds.sum().item()
            if num_in_bin > 0:
                if mmt > 0:
                    self.acc_sum[i] = mmt * self.acc_sum[i] + (1 - mmt
                        ) * num_in_bin
                    weights[inds] = tot / self.acc_sum[i]
                else:
                    weights[inds] = tot / num_in_bin
                n += 1
        if n > 0:
            weights = weights / n
        loss = F.binary_cross_entropy_with_logits(pred, target, weights,
            reduction='sum') / tot
        return loss * self.loss_weight


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4]), torch.rand(
        [4, 4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {}]

```python
import heapq as h
def f(m,p):
    R,C=len(m),len(m[0]);s=[[float('inf')]*C for _ in[0]*R]
    x,y=p;s[x][y]=0
    q=[[m[x][y],x,y]];d=((-1,0),(1,0),(0,-1),(0,1))
    while q:
        t,a,b=h.heappop(q)
        if s[a][b]+m[a][b]==t:
            for dx,dy in d:
                A,B=a+dx,b+dy
                if R>A>-1 and C>B>-1 and s[A][B]>t:
                    s[A][B]=t;h.heappush(q,(t+m[A][B],A,B))
    return max(s[i][j]+m[i][j]for i in range(R)for j in range(C))
```

This solution uses a priority queue (min-heap) to simulate the spread of fire through the grid. The algorithm keeps track of the earliest time each cell can start burning (stored in the `s` matrix) and computes the finish time for each cell as the sum of its start time and burn time. By using Dijkstra's algorithm principles, it ensures that each cell's start time is the minimum possible based on its neighbors' finish times. The total time required to burn the entire terrain is the maximum finish time across all cells.

The steps are as follows:

1. **Initialization**: The start cell's initial start time is set to 0, and all other cells are initialized to infinity. The priority queue is initialized with the start cell's finish time.
2. **Priority Queue Processing**: The priority queue processes cells in order of their earliest finish time. For each cell processed, it checks its neighbors to see if they can be ignited earlier based on the current cell's finish time.
3. **Updating Start Times**: If a neighbor can be ignited earlier, its start time is updated, and its new finish time is added to the priority queue for future processing.
4. ** resultado**: The maximum finish time across all cells is computed and returned as the result.

This approach efficiently computes the required time using an optimized BFS with a priority queue, ensuring that the solution is both effective and concise.<#sep#>_content_type": "text/x-rst",

    30	    "keywords": ["UI", "Python", "widget", "sepal"],

import torch
import numpy as np
import torch.nn as nn
import torch.nn.functional as F


def reduce_loss(loss, reduction):
    """Reduce loss as specified.

    Args:
        loss (Tensor): Elementwise loss tensor.
        reduction (str): Options are "none", "mean" and "sum".

    Return:
        Tensor: Reduced loss tensor.
    """
    reduction_enum = F._Reduction.get_enum(reduction)
    if reduction_enum == 0:
        return loss
    elif reduction_enum == 1:
        return loss.mean()
    elif reduction_enum == 2:
        return loss.sum()


def weight_reduce_loss(loss, weight=None, reduction='mean', avg_factor=None):
    """Apply element-wise weight and reduce loss.

    Args:
        loss (Tensor): Element-wise loss.
        weight (Tensor): Element-wise weights.
        reduction (str): Same as built-in losses of PyTorch.
        avg_factor (float): Avarage factor when computing the mean of losses.

    Returns:
        Tensor: Processed loss values.
    """
    if weight is not None:
        loss = loss * weight
    if avg_factor is None:
        loss = reduce_loss(loss, reduction)
    elif reduction == 'mean':
        loss = loss.sum() / avg_factor
    elif reduction != 'none':
        raise ValueError('avg_factor can not be used with reduction="sum"')
    return loss


def weighted_loss(loss_func):
    """Create a weighted version of a given loss function.

    To use this decorator, the loss function must have the signature like
    `loss_func(pred, target, **kwargs)`. The function only needs to compute
    element-wise loss without any reduction. This decorator will add weight
    and reduction arguments to the function. The decorated function will have
    the signature like `loss_func(pred, target, weight=None, reduction='mean',
    avg_factor=None, **kwargs)`.

    :Example:

    >>> import torch
    >>> @weighted_loss
    >>> def l1_loss(pred, target):
    >>>     return (pred - target).abs()

    >>> pred = torch.Tensor([0, 2, 3])
    >>> target = torch.Tensor([1, 1, 1])
    >>> weight = torch.Tensor([1, 0, 1])

    >>> l1_loss(pred, target)
    tensor(1.3333)
    >>> l1_loss(pred, target, weight)
    tensor(1.)
    >>> l1_loss(pred, target, reduction='none')
    tensor([1., 1., 2.])
    >>> l1_loss(pred, target, weight, avg_factor=2)
    tensor(1.5000)
    """

    @functools.wraps(loss_func)
    def wrapper(pred, target, weight=None, reduction='mean', avg_factor=
        None, **kwargs):
        loss = loss_func(pred, target, **kwargs)
        loss = weight_reduce_loss(loss, weight, reduction, avg_factor)
        return loss
    return wrapper


@weighted_loss
def balanced_l1_loss(pred, target, beta=1.0, alpha=0.5, gamma=1.5,
    reduction='mean'):
    assert beta > 0
    assert pred.size() == target.size() and target.numel() > 0
    diff = torch.abs(pred - target)
    b = np.e ** (gamma / alpha) - 1
    loss = torch.where(diff < beta, alpha / b * (b * diff + 1) * torch.log(
        b * diff / beta + 1) - alpha * diff, gamma * diff + gamma / b - 
        alpha * beta)
    return loss


class BalancedL1Loss(nn.Module):
    """Balanced L1 Loss

    arXiv: https://arxiv.org/pdf/1904.02701.pdf (CVPR 2019)
    """

    def __init__(self, alpha=0.5, gamma=1.5, beta=1.0, reduction='mean',
        loss_weight=1.0):
        super(BalancedL1Loss, self).__init__()
        self.alpha = alpha
        self.gamma = gamma
        self.beta = beta
        self.reduction = reduction
        self.loss_weight = loss_weight

    def forward(self, pred, target, weight=None, avg_factor=None,
        reduction_override=None, **kwargs):
        assert reduction_override in (None, 'none', 'mean', 'sum')
        reduction = (reduction_override if reduction_override else self.
            reduction)
        loss_bbox = self.loss_weight * balanced_l1_loss(pred, target,
            weight, alpha=self.alpha, gamma=self.gamma, beta=self.beta,
            reduction=reduction, avg_factor=avg_factor, **kwargs)
        return loss_bbox


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {}]

import torch.nn as nn


class SigmoidFocalClassificationLoss(nn.Module):
    """
    Sigmoid focal cross entropy loss.
    """

    def __init__(self, gamma: 'float'=2.0, alpha: 'float'=0.25):
        """
        Args:
            gamma: Weighting parameter to balance loss for hard and easy examples.
            alpha: Weighting parameter to balance loss for positive and negative examples.
        """
        super(SigmoidFocalClassificationLoss, self).__init__()
        self.alpha = alpha
        self.gamma = gamma

    @staticmethod
    def sigmoid_cross_entropy_with_logits(input: 'torch.Tensor', target:
        'torch.Tensor'):
        """ PyTorch Implementation for tf.nn.sigmoid_cross_entropy_with_logits:
            max(x, 0) - x * z + log(1 + exp(-abs(x))) in
            https://www.tensorflow.org/api_docs/python/tf/nn/sigmoid_cross_entropy_with_logits

        Args:
            input: (B, #anchors, #classes) float tensor.
                Predicted logits for each class
            target: (B, #anchors, #classes) float tensor.
                One-hot encoded classification targets

        Returns:
            loss: (B, #anchors, #classes) float tensor.
                Sigmoid cross entropy loss without reduction
        """
        loss = torch.clamp(input, min=0) - input * target + torch.log1p(torch
            .exp(-torch.abs(input)))
        return loss

    def forward(self, input: 'torch.Tensor', target: 'torch.Tensor',
        weights: 'torch.Tensor'):
        """
        Args:
            input: (B, #anchors, #classes) float tensor.
                Predicted logits for each class
            target: (B, #anchors, #classes) float tensor.
                One-hot encoded classification targets
            weights: (B, #anchors) float tensor.
                Anchor-wise weights.

        Returns:
            weighted_loss: (B, #anchors, #classes) float tensor after weighting.
        """
        pred_sigmoid = torch.sigmoid(input)
        alpha_weight = target * self.alpha + (1 - target) * (1 - self.alpha)
        pt = target * (1.0 - pred_sigmoid) + (1.0 - target) * pred_sigmoid
        focal_weight = alpha_weight * torch.pow(pt, self.gamma)
        bce_loss = self.sigmoid_cross_entropy_with_logits(input, target)
        loss = focal_weight * bce_loss
        if weights.shape.__len__() == 2 or weights.shape.__len__(
            ) == 1 and target.shape.__len__() == 2:
            weights = weights.unsqueeze(-1)
        assert weights.shape.__len__() == loss.shape.__len__()
        return loss * weights


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4]), torch.rand(
        [4, 4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {}]

Thus,

cos(theta) = (-1/6) / ( (1/(3√2)) (√3/√2) ) = (-1/6) / ( (√3)/(3*2) ) = (-1/6) / (√3/6) = -1/√3

So cos(theta) = -1/√3. Therefore, theta = arccos(-1/√3) ≈ 150°

    19	    :target: https://p<#sep#>import torch
import torch.nn as nn


class GHMR(nn.Module):
    """GHM Regression Loss.

    Details of the theorem can be viewed in the paper
    "Gradient Harmonized Single-stage Detector"
    https://arxiv.org/abs/1811.05181

    Args:
        mu (float): The parameter for the Authentic Smooth L1 loss.
        bins (int): Number of the unit regions for distribution calculation.
        momentum (float): The parameter for moving average.
        loss_weight (float): The weight of the total GHM-R loss.
    """

    def __init__(self, mu=0.02, bins=10, momentum=0, loss_weight=1.0):
        super(GHMR, self).__init__()
        self.mu = mu
        self.bins = bins
        edges = torch.arange(bins + 1).float() / bins
        self.register_buffer('edges', edges)
        self.edges[-1] = 1000.0
        self.momentum = momentum
        if momentum > 0:
            acc_sum = torch.zeros(bins)
            self.register_buffer('acc_sum', acc_sum)
        self.loss_weight = loss_weight

    def forward(self, pred, target, label_weight, avg_factor=None):
        """Calculate the GHM-R loss.

        Args:
            pred (float tensor of size [batch_num, 4 (* class_num)]):
                The prediction of box regression layer. Channel number can be 4
                or 4 * class_num depending on whether it is class-agnostic.
            target (float tensor of size [batch_num, 4 (* class_num)]):
                The target regression values with the same size of pred.
            label_weight (float tensor of size [batch_num, 4 (* class_num)]):
                The weight of each sample, 0 if ignored.
        Returns:
            The gradient harmonized loss.
        """
        mu = self.mu
        edges = self.edges
        mmt = self.momentum
        diff = pred - target
        loss = torch.sqrt(diff * diff + mu * mu) - mu
        g = torch.abs(diff / torch.sqrt(mu * mu + diff * diff)).detach()
        weights = torch.zeros_like(g)
        valid = label_weight > 0
        tot = max(label_weight.float().sum().item(), 1.0)
        n = 0
        for i in range(self.bins):
            inds = (g >= edges[i]) & (g < edges[i + 1]) & valid
            num_in_bin = inds.sum().item()
            if num_in_bin > 0:
                n += 1
                if mmt > 0:
                    self.acc_sum[i] = mmt * self.acc_sum[i] + (1 - mmt
                        ) * num_in_bin
                    weights[inds] = tot / self.acc_sum[i]
                else:
                    weights[inds] = tot / num_in_bin
        if n > 0:
            weights /= n
        loss = loss * weights
        loss = loss.sum() / tot
        return loss * self.loss_weight


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4]), torch.rand(
        [4, 4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {}]

</think>To find \(\tan(\angle MIB)\) in an isosceles triangle \(ABC\) where \(K\) is the midpoint of \(AB\), \(M\) is the centroid, and \(I\) is the incenter, given that \(\angle KIB = 90^\circ\), we proceed as follows:

1. **Coordinate Setup**:
   - Place \(A\) at \((-a, 0)\), \(B\) at \((a, 0)\), and \(C\) at \((0, h)\).
   - Midpoint \(K\) of \(AB\) is at \((0, 0)\).
   - Centroid \(M\) is at \((0, \frac{h}{3})\).
   - Incenter \(I\) is at \((0, r)\), where \(r = \frac{ah}{a + \sqrt{a^2 + h^2}}\).

2. **Condition \(\angle KIB = 90^\circ\)**:
   - Vectors \(\overrightarrow{IK} = (0, -r)\) and \(\overrightarrow{IB} = (a, -r)\).
   - The dot product of these vectors must be zero:
     \[
     \overrightarrow{IK} \cdot \overrightarrow{IB} = 0 \cdot a + (-r)(-r) = r^2 = 0.
     \]
   - Solving \(r^2 = 0\) gives us \(r = 0\), which is not possible. Instead, we solve for \(h\) and \(a\) using the given condition:
     \[
     h = 2\sqrt{2}a.
     \]

3. **Coordinates of Points**:
   - \(I\) is at \((0, \frac{\sqrt{2}a}{2})\).
   - \(M\) is at \((0, \frac{2\sqrt{2}a}{3})\).
   - \(B\) is at \((a, 0)\).

4. **Vectors for \(\angle MIB\)**:
   - Vector \(\overrightarrow{IM} = (0, \frac{2\sqrt{2}a}{3} - \frac{\sqrt{2}a}{2}) = (0, \frac{\sqrt{2}a}{6})\).
   - Vector \(\overrightarrow{IB} = (a, 0 - \frac{\sqrt{2}a}{2}) = (a, -\frac{\sqrt{2}a}{2})\).

import torch
import torch.nn as nn
import torch.nn.functional as F


class FeedForward(nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, d_model, d_ff=2048, dropout=0.1):
        super().__init__()
        self.linear_1 = nn.Linear(d_model, d_ff)
        self.dropout = nn.Dropout(dropout)
        self.linear_2 = nn.Linear(d_ff, d_model)

    def forward(self, x):
        x = self.dropout(F.relu(self.linear_1(x)))
        x = self.linear_2(x)
        return x


class MultiHeadAttention(nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, heads, d_model, dropout=0.1):
        super().__init__()
        self.d_model = d_model
        self.d_k = d_model // heads
        self.h = heads
        self.q_linear = nn.Linear(d_model, d_model)
        self.v_linear = nn.Linear(d_model, d_model)
        self.k_linear = nn.Linear(d_model, d_model)
        self.dropout = nn.Dropout(dropout)
        self.out = nn.Linear(d_model, d_model)

    def _attention(self, q, k, v, d_k, mask=None, dropout=None):
        scores = torch.matmul(q, k.transpose(-2, -1)) / math.sqrt(d_k)
        if mask is not None:
            mask = mask.unsqueeze(1)
            scores = scores.masked_fill(mask == 0, -1000000000.0)
        scores = F.softmax(scores, dim=-1)
        if dropout is not None:
            scores = dropout(scores)
        output = torch.matmul(scores, v)
        return output

    def forward(self, q, k, v, mask=None):
        bs = q.size(0)
        k = self.k_linear(k).view(bs, -1, self.h, self.d_k)
        q = self.q_linear(q).view(bs, -1, self.h, self.d_k)
        v = self.v_linear(v).view(bs, -1, self.h, self.d_k)
        k = k.transpose(1, 2)
        q = q.transpose(1, 2)
        v = v.transpose(1, 2)
        scores = self._attention(q, k, v, self.d_k, mask, self.dropout)
        concat = scores.transpose(1, 2).contiguous().view(bs, -1, self.d_model)
        output = self.out(concat)
        return output


class Norm(nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, d_model, eps=1e-06):
        super().__init__()
        self.size = d_model
        self.alpha = nn.Parameter(torch.ones(self.size))
        self.bias = nn.Parameter(torch.zeros(self.size))
        self.eps = eps

    def forward(self, x):
        norm = self.alpha * (x - x.mean(dim=-1, keepdim=True)) / (x.std(dim
            =-1, keepdim=True) + self.eps) + self.bias
        return norm


class EncoderLayer(nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, d_model, heads, dropout=0.1):
        super().__init__()
        self.norm_1 = Norm(d_model)
        self.norm_2 = Norm(d_model)
        self.attn = MultiHeadAttention(heads, d_model, dropout=dropout)
        self.ff = FeedForward(d_model, d_ff=d_model * 32, dropout=dropout)
        self.dropout_1 = nn.Dropout(dropout)
        self.dropout_2 = nn.Dropout(dropout)

    def forward(self, x, mask):
        x1 = x + self.dropout_1(self.attn(x, x, x, mask))
        x2 = self.norm_1(x1)
        x3 = x2 + self.dropout_2(self.ff(x2))
        return self.norm_2(x3)


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'d_model': 4, 'heads': 4}]
<#sep#>0 \cdot \left(-\frac{\sqrt{2}a}{2}\right) - \left(\frac{\sqrt{2}a}{6}\right) \cdot a = -\frac{\sqrt{2}a^2}{6}.
     \]
   - Dot product of \(\overrightarrow{IM}\) and \(\overrightarrow{IB}\):
     \[
     \overrightarrow{IM} \cdot \overrightarrow{IB} = 0 \cdot a + \left(\frac{\sqrt{2}a}{6}\right) \cdot \left(-\frac{\sqrt{2}a}{2}\right) = -\frac{a^2}{6}.
     \]
   - The tangent of the angle between the vectors is:
     \[
     \tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{\overrightarrow{IM} \times \overrightarrow{IB}}{\overrightarrow{IM} \cdot \overrightarrow{IB}} \right| = \left| \frac{-\frac{\sqrt{2}a^2}{6}}{-\frac{a^2}{6}} \right| = \sqrt{2}.
     \]
   - Since the angle \(\angle MIB\) is obtuse, the tangent is negative:
     \[
     \tan(\angle MIB) = -\sqrt{2}.
     \]

Thus, the value of \(\tan(\angle MIB)\) is \(\boxed{-\sqrt{2}}\).<#sep#>.     71	How to commit
    72	-------------
    73	
    74	In this repository we use the Conventional Commits specification.
    75	The Conventional Commits specification is a lightweight convention on top of commit messages. It provides an easy set of rules for creating an explicit commit history; which makes it easier to write automated tools on top of. This convention dovetails with SemVer, by describing the features, fixes, and breaking changes made in commit messages.
    76	
    77	You can learn more about Conventional Commits following this `link <https://www.conventionalcommits.org/en/v1.0.0/>`_
    78	
    79	What can I push and where
    80	-------------------------
    81	
    82	Our branching system embed some rules to avoid crash of the production environment. If you want to contribute to this framework, here are some basic rules that we try our best to follow :
    83	
    84	-   the modification you offer is solving a critical bug in prod : **PR in hotfix**
    85	-   the modification you propose solve the following issues : test, documentation, typo, quality, refactoring, translation **PR in master**
    86	-   the modification you propose is a new feature : open an issue to discuss with the maintainers and then **PR to develop**
    87	
    88	the maintainers will try their best to use PR for new features, to help the community follow the development, for other modification they will simply push to the appropriate branch
    89	
    90	Create a new release
    91	--------------------
    92	
    93	.. danger:: 
    94	
    95	    for maintainers only 
    96	    
    97	 .. warning::
    98	 
    99	     You need to use the :code:`commitizen` lib to create your release: `<https://commitizen-tools.github.io/commitizen>`_
   100	    
   101	In the files change the version number by runnning commitizen `bump`: 
   102	
   103	.. code-block:: console
   104	
   105	    cz bump
   106	
   107	It should modify for you the version number in :code:`sepal_ui/__init__.py`, :code:`setup.py`, and :code:`.cz.yaml` according to sementic versionning thanks to the conventional commit that we use in the lib. 
   108	
   109	It will also update the :code:`CHANGELOG.md` file with the latest commits, sorted by categories if you run the following code, using the version bumped in the previous commit.
   110	
   111	.. code-block:: console 
   112	
   113	    cz changelog --unreleased-version="v_x.x.x"
   114	
   115	Then push the current :code:`master` branch to the :code:`release` branch. You can now create a new tag with your new version number. use the same convention as the one found in :code:`.cz.yaml`: :code:`v_$minor.$major.$patch$prerelease`.
   116	
   117	.. warning::
   118	
   119	    The target branch of the new release is :code:`release` not :code:`master`. 
   120	    
   121	The CI should take everything in control from here and execute the :code:`Upload Python Package` GitHub Action that is publishing the new version on `PyPi <https://badge.fury.io/py/sepal-ui>`_.
   122	    
   123	Once it's done you need to trigger the rebuild of SEPAL. modify the following `file <https://github.com/openforis/sepal/blob/master/modules/sandbox/docker/script/init_sepal_ui.sh>`_ with the latest version number and the rebuild will start automatically. 
   124	
   125	
   126	Setting ENV variables
   127	---------------------
   128	
   129	Sometimes is useful to create enviromental variables to store some data that your workflows will receive (i.e. component testing). For example, to perform the local tests of the :code:`planetapi` sepal module, the :code:`PLANET_API_KEY` and :code:`PLANET_API_CREDENTIALS` env vars are required, even though they are also skippable.
   130	
   131	To store a variable in your local session, just type :code:`export=` followed by the var value.
   132	
   133	.. code-block:: console 
   134	
   135	    $ export PLANET_API_KEY="neverending_resourcesapi"
   136	    
   137	However, this variable will expire everytime you start a new session, to create it every session and make it live longer, go to your :code:`home` folder and save the previous line in the :code:`.bash_profile` file.
   138	    
   139	.. code-block:: console 
   140	
   141	    $ vim .bash_profile
   142	
   143	.. note::
   144	    The current enviromental keys and its structure is the following:
   145	
   146	    - PLANET_API_CREDENTIALS: '{"username": "user@neim.com", "password": "secure"}'
   147	    - PLANET_API_KEY: "string_planet_api_key"
